## I / 一

===Original===
When the clergyman had gone, the bride turned.
Before her was an open window before which was the open sea. In the air was a tropical languor, a savour of brine, the scent of lilies, the sound of mandolins that are far away. Below, in the garden, were masses of scarlet, high heaps of geranium blooms. A bit beyond was the Caprian blue of the San Diego Bay. There, a yacht rode, white and spacious. The yacht belonged to her husband who was beside her. She turned again and as passionately he embraced her; she coloured.
For the moment, as they stood there, they seemed so sheerly dissimilar that they might have come of alien races, from different zones. He, with his fair hair, his fair skin, his resolute and aggressive face, was typically Anglo-Saxon. She, with her delicate features, her dense black hair, and disquieting eyes, looked like a Madrilene Madonna﻿—one of those fascinating and slightly shocking creations of seventeenth-century art that more nearly resemble infantas serenaded by caballeros than queens of the sky. There was a deeper contrast. He appeared frankly material; she, all soul.
Leisurely she freed herself.
===Chinese===
牧师离去后，新娘转过身来。
她面前是一扇敞开的窗，窗外便是开阔的海。空气里有一种热带的慵懒、一缕海盐的气息、百合的芬芳，还有远处传来的曼陀林声。下方花园里是一片一片的猩红，天竺葵的花朵堆得高高的。再远些，是圣迭戈湾那卡普里（Capri）式的湛蓝。湾上泊着一艘游艇，洁白而宽敞。艇是她身旁丈夫的。她又转过身，他同样热烈地拥抱她；她红了脸。
此刻两人并肩立在那里，看上去截然不同得仿佛出自异族、来自不同的地带。他金发白肤，面容果决而咄咄逼人，是典型的盎格鲁-撒克逊人。她五官纤秀，一头浓密的黑发，一双令人不安的眼睛，像一尊马德里（Madrid）圣母——十七世纪艺术中那类迷人而又略微骇俗的造物，与其说像天空的女王，不如说更像被骑士们簇拥着唱小夜曲的西班牙公主。还有一层更深的反差：他坦坦荡荡是物质的；她，通身是灵魂。
她从容地脱出身来。

===Original===
“One might know,” she began, then paused. A smile completed the sentence.
He smiled too.
“Yes, Leilah, one might know that however I hold you to me, I never can hold you enough.”
“And I! I could be held by you forever.”
On the door came a tap, rapid and assured. A page entered, the preoccupation of the tip in his face, in his hand a platter of letters.
The man, taking the letters, dismissed him.
===Chinese===
“这么说来，”她开了口，随即顿住。一个微笑替她补全了这句话。
他也微笑。
“是啊，蕾拉，谁都看得出来：无论我把你搂得多紧，也永远搂不够。”
“我也是！被你搂一辈子都愿意。”
门上响起一记敲击，急促而笃定。一个小听差走了进来，满脸心思都悬在赏钱上，手里托着一盘信件。
男人接过信，把他打发走了。

===Original===
“Miss Ogston,” he continued. “From your father, confound him. It is the last time he will address you in that fashion. Miss Ogston,” he repeated. “From the Silverstairs, I fancy. Gulian Verplank. There is but one for me.”
He looked at his watch. “The launch from the yacht will be here shortly.”
“When do we start?”
“Whenever you like. The Marquesas will keep. Bora-Bora will be the same whenever we get there. Only﻿—”
“Only what?”
“I am in love with you, not with hotels.”
===Chinese===
“奥格斯顿小姐，”他接着念，“你父亲寄来的，这家伙。这是他最后一次那样称呼你了。奥格斯顿小姐，”他又念了一遍，“我看是西尔弗斯泰尔斯家的。格里安·弗普兰克。给我只有一封。”
他看了看表。“艇上的小艇一会儿就到。”
“我们什么时候动身？”
“随你喜欢。马克萨斯群岛（the Marquesas）跑不了。博拉博拉岛（Bora-Bora）也一样，我们几时到，它几时都是老样子。只是——”
“只是什么？”
“我爱上的是你，不是旅馆。”

===Original===
“Let us go then. There will be a moon tonight?”
“A new one, a honeymoon, a honeymoon begun.”
“Gulian! As if it could end!”
In pronouncing the *u* in his name her mouth made the sketch of a kiss.
“You would not wish it to?” he asked.
“When I die, perhaps, and even then only to be continued hereafter. Heaven would not be heaven without you.”
===Chinese===
“那我们走吧。今晚会有月亮吧？”
“一轮新月，一个蜜月，一个开了头的蜜月。”
“格里安！说得好像它会有完似的！”
念他名字里那个 *u* 音时，她的唇形勾出一个吻的轮廓。
“你不愿它有完？”他问。
“等我死的时候吧，也许；即便那时，也只是换个地方续下去。没有你，天堂也不成其为天堂。”

===Original===
She spoke slowly, with little pauses, in a manner that differed from his own mode of speech, which was quick and forceful.
Verplank turned to the letter that had been addressed to him, and which he still held. Without opening it, he tore it into long, thin strips. It was, he knew from the imprint, a communication of no importance; but, at the moment, the action seemed a reply to her remark. It served to indicate his complete indifference to everything and everyone save her only. Afterward, with a regret that was to be eternal, she wished he had done the same with hers.
Yet, pleased at the time, she smiled.
“Gulian, you do love me, but I wonder do you love me as absolutely as I love you?”
Verplank, with a gesture that was familiar to him, closed and opened a hand.
“I do not know. But while I think you cannot love me more wholly than I love you, I do know that to me you are the unique.”
===Chinese===
她说话缓慢，带着小小的停顿，与他的说话方式迥异——他语速快，斩钉截铁。
弗普兰克转向那封写给他、此刻仍拿在他手里的信。他不曾拆开，便把它撕成长长的细条。从印戳他知道，那不过是无关紧要的函件；然而在此刻，这一举动仿佛是对她那句话的回答，足以表明除她之外，他对万事万物、对一切人等何等全然漠然。事后，她怀着一种绵延无尽的悔恨，只愿他当时对她那些信也如法炮制。
不过她当时满心欢喜，笑了。
“格里安，你确实爱我，可我不免想问：你爱我爱得可有我爱你那样绝对？”
弗普兰克做了一个他惯常的手势，一只手握拢又张开。
“我不知道。但我想你爱我不会比我爱你更彻底，我却确实知道：于我，你是独一无二的那一个。”

===Original===
Leilah moved to where he stood.
“Gulian, and you to me. You are the only one.” She moved closer. Raising her hands, she put them on his shoulders. “Tell me, shall you be long away?”
“An hour or two. Apropos, would you care to leave before dinner?”
“Yes.”
“We will dine on board, then. Is there anything in particular you would like?”
“Yes, lilies, plenty of lilies; and pineapples; and the sound of your voice.”
===Chinese===
蕾拉走到他站的地方。
“格里安，你于我也是。你是唯一的。”她又凑近了些，抬起双手，搭上他的肩头。“告诉我，你要离开很久吗？”
“一两个钟头。对了，你愿意在晚饭前动身吗？”
“愿意。”
“那我们就在船上用晚餐。有什么你特别想要的吗？”
“有，百合，好多好多的百合；还有菠萝；还有你说话的声音。”

===Original===
Lifting her hands from his shoulders to his face, she drew it to her own. Their lips met longly. With the savour of her about him, Verplank passed out.
Idly Leilah turned. Before her the sea lay, a desert of blue. Below, on the beach, it broke with a boom in high white waves which, in retreating, became faintly mauve. The spectacle charmed her. But other scenes effaced it; sudden pictures of the Marquesas; the long flight southward; the brief, bright days; the nights that would be briefer still. Pleasurably for a while these things detained her. Idly again she turned.
On the table were the letters. One was from an intimate friend, Violet Silverstairs, a New York girl who had married an Englishman, and who since then had resided abroad. The other was﻿—or appeared to be﻿—from Matlack Ogston.
Matlack Ogston was Leilah’s father. That a father should write to a daughter is only natural. But that this father should write surprised her, as already it had surprised Verplank. When he mentioned whom the letter was from she had thought he must be in error. Now, as she opened it, she found that he had been. Her father had not written. The envelope contained a second envelope addressed to another person. This envelope had formerly been sealed and since been opened. It held three letters in an unknown hand.
She began at one of them. More exactly, she began, as some women do begin, at the end. The signature startled. At once, as she turned to the initial sentences, she experienced the curious and unenviable sensation of falling from an inordinate height, and it was not with any idea that the sensation would cease, but rather with the craving to know, which in certain crises of the emotions becomes more unendurable than any uncertainty can be, that she read the rest of the first letter; after it, the second letter, and the third.
Then, as truth stared at her and she at truth, so monstrous was its aspect that, with one shuddering intake of the breath, life withered within her, light vanished without.
===Chinese===
她把双手从他肩上移到他脸上，将那张脸引向自己。两人的唇久久相贴。带着萦绕周身的她的气息，弗普兰克走了出去。
蕾拉慵懒地转过身。她面前铺着海，一片蓝色的荒漠。下方滩头，海轰然迸碎成高高的白浪，退去时又淡成浅浅的紫红。这景象令她心醉。可别的画面又把它抹去了：马克萨斯群岛骤然浮现的图景；向南的长长航程；短促而明亮的白昼；还要更短的黑夜。这些念头愉悦地缠了她一阵。她又慵懒地转过身去。
桌上放着那些信。一封来自她的密友维奥莱特·西尔弗斯泰尔斯（Violet Silverstairs），一个嫁给了英国人、此后便侨居海外的纽约姑娘。另一封——或者说是看似——出自马特拉克·奥格斯顿（Matlack Ogston）。
马特拉克·奥格斯顿是蕾拉的父亲。父亲给女儿写信，本属寻常。可这位父亲居然写起信来，却令她诧异，正如方才也令弗普兰克诧异一样。他说出寄信人是谁时，她还以为他必定弄错了。此刻拆开信，她发现他确实弄错了。她的父亲没有写信。信封里套着第二只信封，收信的却是另一个人。那只信封原先封过口，后来又被拆开了。里面装着三封信，笔迹陌生。
她从其中一封开始。更确切地说，她像某些女人那样，从末尾开始。署名让她心头一惊。她随即翻到开头几句，立刻体验到一种古怪而无人艳羡的感觉：正从极高空坠落。她读下去，并非指望这感觉会止住，而是出于想知道的渴念——在情感的某些危机里，这渴念比任何悬而未决都更难忍受。她读完了第一封余下的部分；接着是第二封；然后是第三封。
随后，真相瞪视着她，她也瞪视着真相；其面目狰狞至极，她打了个寒噤，猛吸一口气，体内的生命随之枯萎，身外的光明随之熄灭。

===Original===
When ultimately, without knowing who she was; when, conscious only of an objective self struggling in darkness with the intangible and the void, when then life and light returned, she was on the floor, the monster peering at her.
She disowned it, disavowed it. But beside her on the floor the letters lay. There was its lair. It had sprung from them, and always from them it would be peering at her, driving her mad with its blighting eyes, unless﻿—
She got on her hands and knees, and from them to her feet. Her body ached from the fall, and her head was throbbing. With the idea that smelling salts, or some cologne water which she had, might help her, she went and fetched them from an adjoining room. They were not of much use, she found, though presently she could think more clearly, and in a little while she was considering the possibility that had loomed.
In certain conditions the soul gets used to monsters. It makes itself at home with what it must. Her soul, she thought, might also. But even as she thought it, she knew she never could. She knew that even were she able to succeed in blinding herself to this thing by day, at night it would crawl to her, sit at her side, pluck at her sleeve, wake her, and cry: “Behold me!”
It would cry it at her until she cried it at him. Then inevitably it would kill her.
She had been seated, bathing her head with cologne. Now fear, helplessness, the consciousness of both possessed her. They impelled her to act. She stood up. She looked about the room. Filled with flowers and sunshine, it said nothing. Beyond was the sea. It called to her. It told her that in a rowboat she could drift and be lost. It told her that that night she could throw herself from the yacht. The blue expanse, the high white waves, the little mauve ripples invited.
===Chinese===
最终，当她已不知自己是谁；当她只意识到一个客观的自我正在黑暗中与无形与虚空搏斗；当生命与光明随之归来时，她已倒在地板上，那怪物正俯身窥视着她。
她不认它，否认它。可她身旁的地板上正摊着那几封信。那是它的巢穴。它是从信里窜出来的，还将永远从信里窜出来窥伺她，用那双摧心蚀骨的眼睛逼她发疯，除非——
她撑起双手双膝，又从跪姿站起。摔得浑身作痛，头也突突直跳。想到嗅盐、或是她备着的一点古龙水兴许管用，她便到隔壁房间取了来。她发觉用处不大，不过渐渐地理智清楚些了，不多时，她已在思量那个隐然浮现的可能。
在某些境况下，灵魂是会习惯怪物的。它总得与不得不相处的东西处得像在自己家里。她的灵魂，她想，或许也能。可这念头刚起，她就知道自己永远不能。她知道，即便她能在白昼侥幸对这东西视而不见，一到夜里它也会爬到她跟前，坐在她身边，扯她的衣袖，把她弄醒，喊道：“看着我！”
它会冲她喊，直到她冲它喊出来。那时，它必然把她杀死。
她原本坐着，用古龙水浸着额头。此刻，恐惧与无助，以及对二者的自觉，一并攫住了她。它们驱策她行动。她站起身，环视房间。满室花影天光，房间一言不发。外面是海。海在召唤她。它告诉她，乘一叶划艇便可随波漂去，就此失踪；它告诉她，当夜她可以从游艇上一跃而下。蓝色的浩渺，高高的白浪，细细的紫红涟漪，都在殷勤相邀。

===Original===
The room, though, with its flowers and sunshine, deterred. To throw herself from the yacht meant that she would have to wait. It meant more. It meant that she would have to see him. It meant that she would have to feign and pretend. These things she could not do.
There remained the rowboat. Yet, in some way, now, the sea seemed less inviting. At the thought of its embrace and of its depths she shrank. To die, to cease anymore to be, to succumb like the heroines of the old tragedies to fate, at the idea of that, her young soul revolted. There must be some other course.
She looked from the window. Beneath, before the ocean, a motor was passing. The whirr of it prompting, flight occurred to her, an escape to some spot that would engulf her as surely as the waves. Hesitatingly she considered it. But there was nothing else. Moreover, if she were to go, she must go at once.
She turned, crossed the room, stooped, gathered the letters, and seated herself at the table. There she put the letters in another envelope which she addressed to Verplank. While writing his name, her hand trembled, it shook on the paper drops of ink. These she tried to blot, and made a smear.
Trembling still, she got up, went to the telephone, and attempted to speak. At first, too overcome to do so, she leaned against the wall. It did not seem possible that she could do this thing. But she must, she knew. At last, with an effort, she spoke.
“When does the next train leave San Diego? One moment. Have my servants sent to me; my servants, yes, and﻿—and order a motor.”
===Chinese===
然而房间，连同它的花与天光，却在劝阻。从游艇投海，意味着她得等待。还不止如此。意味着她得见他。意味着她得敷衍，得装假。这些她做不到。
还剩划艇这条路。可不知怎么，此刻海显得不那么诱人了。一想到它的怀抱、它的深处，她便畏缩。去死，从此不复存在，像旧日悲剧里的女主人公那样向命运低头——想到这里，她年轻的灵魂反抗起来。总该还有别的路。
她朝窗外望去。下方，临海的地方，一辆汽车正驶过。那嗡嗡声提示着她，出走的念头油然而生——逃往某个足以吞没她的去处，如波涛一样确凿。她迟疑地思量着。可再没有别的了。况且，要走就得立刻走。
她转过身，穿过房间，俯身拾起信件，在桌前坐下。她把信装进另一只信封，封面写上弗普兰克收。写他名字时她的手抖着，墨水在纸上滴落点点。她想吸干墨迹，反而抹成一团污渍。
她仍在发抖，起身走到电话机前，想要开口。起初她撑不住，说不出话，便靠在墙上。做这样一件事，看来不可能是她做得出来的。可她明白，她必须。终于，她奋力开了口。
“下一班火车几点离开圣迭戈（San Diego）？等一下。叫我的仆人来见我；我的仆人，对，还有——还有叫一辆汽车。”

===Original===
Again she leaned against the wall. The room had become intolerable. Into the languors of the air a suffocation had entered, and it was unconsciously, in a condition semi-somnambulistic that she found herself considering the pink of the ceiling, then the rose-leaves woven in the green of the carpet, the dull red of the table-cover, the darker red of a tassel, the tall vase that stood on the table and in which were taller lilies.
There, beneath them was the monster. Its vibrations, disseminating through the room, were silhouetted on the walls. She could not see them, but she could feel them. They choked her.
But now her servants appeared. Nervously, with an irritability so foreign to her, that they eyed each other uncertainly, she gave them hurried commands. These obeyed and the porters summoned, she passed, choking still, from this room, the secrets of which the walls detained.
It was perhaps preordered that they should do so. Long later, in looking back, she realised that destiny then was having its say with her, and realised also why. At the time, however, she was ignorant of two incidents, which, after the fashion of the apparently insignificant, subsequently became the reverse.
One incident had the porters for agent; the other was effected by a maid who supervened. The porters, in removing the luggage, collided with the table. The inkstand, the tall vase with the taller lilies, were upset; the vase, spilling water and flowers, fell broken on the floor; from the stand, ink rippled on the red of the cloth, on the darker red of the tassels, on the envelope which Leilah had directed to Verplank. These things, a maid, summoned by the crash, removed.
When Verplank returned, the table was bare.
===Chinese===
她又靠到墙上。房间已变得难以忍受。空气的慵懒里渗进了一股窒息。不知不觉间，她处在半梦游的状态里，发觉自己正打量着天花板的粉红，接着是织进地毯绿意里的玫瑰叶纹，桌布的暗红，流苏的深红，桌上立着的高花瓶，瓶里是更高的百合。
就在那底下，是那怪物。它的震荡散布全屋，把黑影投在墙上。她看不见，却感觉得到。它们扼着她的喉咙。
这时她的仆人们来了。她神色紧张，带着一种与她全然相悖的暴躁，匆匆发号施令，令他们迟疑不定地面面相觑。仆人们遵命而行，脚夫也叫来了，她仍觉窒息地走出了这个房间——这个墙壁扣留着其秘密的房间。
墙壁扣留秘密，或许本是预先注定的。很久以后回头看，她才明白命运当时正在对她发话，也明白了缘由。可那时她并不知道两件小事，而这两件事，一如所有看似无关紧要者的惯例，后来恰恰反了过来。
一件小事以脚夫为执行者；另一件由随后赶到的一名女仆完成。脚夫们搬行李时撞上了桌子。墨水台连同插着高百合的高花瓶一齐翻倒；花瓶泼着水和花，摔碎在地板上；墨水从台面上漾开，洇过桌布的红、流苏的深红，也洇过蕾拉写明弗普兰克收的那只信封。这些东西，被响声唤来的女仆收拾掉了。
弗普兰克回来时，桌上已空空如也。

===Original===
He did not notice. What he alone noticed was Leilah’s absence. She is below, he told himself. Then precisely as she had summoned her servants, he summoned his.
“Roberts,” he said presently to a man. “Find Mrs. Verplank. Then get my things together. We start at once.”
For a moment the man considered the master. At once civilly but stolidly he spoke:
“Mrs. Verplank has gone, sir.”
Verplank, who had turned on his heel, turned back.
“What?”
===Chinese===
他没有留意。他唯一留意到的是蕾拉不在了。她在楼下，他对自己说。于是，恰如她方才唤来仆人那样，他也唤来了自己的。
“罗伯茨（Roberts），”他随即对一名男仆说，“去找弗普兰克夫人。然后把我的东西收拾齐。我们即刻动身。”
男仆打量了主人片刻，随即客气而木然地说：
“弗普兰克夫人已经走了，先生。”
弗普兰克本已转身要走，闻言又转了回来。
“什么？”

===Original===
“The hotel is full of it, sir. When I found that Mrs. Verplank was leaving, I﻿—”
“What!” Verplank, in angry amazement, repeated.
“Mrs. Verplank is taking the limited, sir. It was the clerk who told me.”
Then, for a moment, the master considered the man. At the simple statement his mind had become like a sea in a storm. A whirlwind tossed his thoughts.
But Leilah was still too near, her caresses were too recent for him to be able to realise that she had actually gone, and the fact that he could not realise it disclosed itself in those words which all have uttered, all at least before whom the inexplicable has sprung:
“It is impossible!”
===Chinese===
“旅馆里都在传这件事，先生。我发现弗普兰克夫人要走的时候，我——”
“什么！”弗普兰克又惊又怒，重复了一遍。
“弗普兰克夫人乘的是特快列车，先生。是办事员告诉我的。”
这回轮到主人打量男仆片刻。这句平平淡淡的话，已让他的头脑变成风暴中的海。一阵旋风把他的思绪抛来掷去。
可蕾拉还太切近，她的爱抚还太新鲜，他无法意识到她真的走了。这份无法意识，泄在了那句话里——人人说过的那句话，至少，凡有不可思议之事当面跳出的，人人都说过：
“这不可能！”

===Original===
“Yes, sir, it does seem most unusual.”
Verplank had spoken less to the man than to himself, and for a moment stood engrossed in that futilest of human endeavours, the effort to read a riddle of which the only Oedipus is time.
At once all the imaginable causes that could have contributed to it danced before him and vanished. He told himself that Leilah’s disappearance might be an attempt at some hide-and-go-seek which shortly would end. But he knew her to be incapable of such nonsense. Immediately he decided that his servant was in error, and that she was then on the yacht. If not, then, clearly she had gone mad, or else﻿—
But there are certain hypotheses which certain intellects decline to stomach. Yet the letter from her father recurring to him, he did consider the possibility that she might have gone because of some secret of his bachelor life. Anything may be distorted. Unfolded by her father, these secrets, which in themselves were not very dark, might be made to look infernal, and could readily be so made by this man who was not only just the one to do it, but who would have an object in so doing. Always he had been inimical to Verplank, and this, the abandoned bridegroom then felt, not on his account, but because of his father.
The latter, Effingham Verplank, had been a great catch, and a great beau. His charm had been myrrh and cassia﻿—and nightshade, as well﻿—to many women, among others to an aunt of Leilah, Hilda Hemingway, whose husband had called him out, called him abroad, rather, where the too charming Verplank waited until Hemingway fired, and then shot in the air. He considered that the gentlemanly thing to do. He was, perhaps, correct. But perhaps, too, it was hardly worth while to go abroad to do it. Yet, however that may be, the attitude of the injured husband, while no doubt equally correct, was less debonair. He obtained a divorce.
The matter created an enormous scandal, in the sedater days when New York society was a small and early family party and scandals were passing rare. But, like everything else, it was forgotten, even, and perhaps particularly by the parties directly concerned. Hemingway married again; the precarious Hilda married also; the too charming Verplank vacated the planet, and his widow went a great deal into the world.
===Chinese===
“是的，先生，这看上去确实很不寻常。”
弗普兰克这话说给自己的成分多于说给男仆。他怔立片刻，沉溺于人类最徒劳的一桩营生里：解一个谜，而这谜唯一的俄狄浦斯（Oedipus）是时间。
刹那间，一切想得到的缘由都在他眼前舞过，又纷纷消散。他告诉自己，蕾拉的失踪也许是一场捉迷藏，很快便会收场。可他清楚她决不会闹这种孩子气。随即他断定是仆人弄错了，她此刻正在艇上。若不然，那她显然是疯了，再不然——
但总有些假说，是某些头脑拒绝下咽的。然而，她父亲的那封信重回心头，他倒真的思量起一种可能：她也许是因他独身岁月里的某个秘密而走的。任何事都可能被歪曲。这些秘密本身并不怎么阴暗，可经她父亲一一道出，便可能被渲染得形同地狱；而由这个男人来做，不但是再合适不过的人选，他也有这样做的动机。他向来敌视弗普兰克，这位被撇下的新郎当时感到，这份敌意不是冲他来的，而是冲他父亲去的。
后者，埃芬厄姆·弗普兰克（Effingham Verplank），当年是个抢手的佳婿，也是个出名的花花公子。他的魅力对许多女人是没药与肉桂——也是颠茄——其中便有蕾拉的一位姨母希尔达·海明威（Hilda Hemingway）。她的丈夫向他下了战书——确切说，是约他去国外——在国外，这位魅力过人的弗普兰克等着海明威开枪，然后朝天放了一枪。他认为那才是绅士的做法。他或许没错。可或许，为这一点专程出国也不值当。然而无论如何，那位受辱丈夫的态度，虽无疑同样无可指摘，却少了几分洒脱。他离成了婚。
这件事掀起了一场轩然大波，那还是纽约社交界不过是一场小小的家宴、散场散得早，丑闻犹属罕见的年头。但正如一切世事，它也被淡忘了，甚至——或许恰恰是——被当事诸方淡忘。海明威续了弦；岌岌可危的希尔达也改了嫁；那位魅力过人的弗普兰克辞别人世，他的遗孀则大大方方地出入起社交场来。

===Original===
This lady had accepted the scandal, as she had accepted many another, with a serenity that was really beautiful. But, then, her seductive husband had always seemed to her so perfectly irresistible, so created to conquer, that﻿—as their son afterward found it necessary to explain﻿—it no more occurred to her to sit in judgment on his victims, than it occurred to her to sit on him. With not only philosophic wisdom, but in the true spirit of Christian charity, she overlooked it all.
The culminant episode in the matter﻿—the death of the volatile Verplank﻿—took place at an hour when his son was too young to be more than aware that his father had been taken away in a box. Leilah was even less advanced. It was years before she learned of her aunt’s delinquencies. When she did, that lady had also passed away, as had previously passed a child of hers, one that, perhaps, did not belong to her first husband, and, certainly not to her second, the result being that, in default of other heirs, she left a fortune to Leilah, whose mother had left her another.
When her mother died, Leilah was in the nursery. Her father, who thereafter abandoned her to servants and governesses, she seldom saw. When she did see him, he ignored her completely. It was a way he had. He ignored also and quite as completely the son of the deadly Verplank.
To make up for it, or it may be to make trouble, the boy’s mother never regarded Leilah otherwise than with that smile of sweet approbation with which she gratified all the world﻿—all the world, that is, save those only who were not in hers. Among the gratified were the Arlington girls, two beauties, of whom the elder, Violet, was Leilah’s closest friend.
It was at Newport, at Violet’s wedding to Silverstairs, a young Englishman who had followed her from Europe, and who at once took her back there; it was at this ceremony, in which Leilah participated as bridesmaid, and Verplank as best man, it was then that both became aware of a joint desire. It seemed to them that they were born to love each other, to love always, forever. Forever!﻿—in a world where all things must end, and do. But the eagerness of it was upon them. Leilah wrote to her father. Verplank wrote to him also.
Matlack Ogston ignored Verplank’s letter as invariably he had ignored Verplank. His daughter’s he promptly returned. Across it was scrawled one word. That word was “No.”
===Chinese===
这位夫人接受这桩丑闻，一如她接受过的许多别的丑闻，那份从容实在漂亮。不过话说回来，在她眼里，她那迷人的丈夫永远那么令人无法抗拒，天生就是要征服人的，以致——正如他们的儿子后来觉得有必要解释的那样——她从没想到要审判他的牺牲品，正如从没想到要审判他本人。她不但以哲人的智慧，而且本着基督徒仁爱的真精神，把这一切全宽恕了。
这件事的收场——那位轻浮无常的弗普兰克之死——发生在他儿子年纪还太小的时候，小到只晓得父亲被装在一只箱子里抬走了。蕾拉那时更懵懂。多年之后她才听说姨母的失德。而等她听说时，那位夫人也已故去，此前故去的还有她的一个孩子——那孩子或许不是她前夫的骨血，也肯定不是她第二任丈夫的；结果是，因别无继承人，她把一笔财产留给了蕾拉，而蕾拉的母亲此前也已留给她一笔。
母亲去世时，蕾拉还在育婴室的年纪。父亲此后把她丢给仆人和家庭女教师，她难得见他一面。偶尔见到，他也全然当她不存在。这是他的一贯做派。对那位要命的弗普兰克的儿子，他也同样全然视若无睹。
作为补偿——也可能是为了添乱——那男孩的母亲对蕾拉始终报以那种甜美的嘉许微笑，那微笑曾取悦过整个世界——也就是说，整个世界，唯独除外那些不在她的世界里的。蒙她取悦的有阿灵顿家（Arlington）的两位小姐，一对美人，姐姐维奥莱特是蕾拉最亲密的朋友。
那是在纽波特，在维奥莱特与西尔弗斯泰尔斯的婚礼上——那年轻英国人从欧洲一路追随她而来，婚后又立刻带她回了欧洲；正是在那场典礼上，蕾拉做伴娘，弗普兰克做傧相，正是在那时，两人都察觉了同一个心愿。他们觉得彼此生来就是要相爱的，要爱到永远，永远。永远！——在这个万物必有尽、且真有尽的世界里。可那份热切已攫住了他们。蕾拉给父亲写信。弗普兰克也给他写了信。
马特拉克·奥格斯顿对弗普兰克的信置之不理，一如既往地无视弗普兰克其人。女儿的信他当即退回，信面上潦草地涂着一个字。那个字是“不”。

===Original===
Interests more commonplace had meanwhile transported Verplank from Newport to San Francisco. Informed of the veto, which to Leilah was an incentive and to him an affront, he had wired her to meet him at Coronado, this resort in Southern California which together they had been preparing to leave.
The night previous, on a yacht chartered at the Golden Gate, Verplank had arrived. It was by train, the next morning, that Leilah had come. The wedding followed. Before them lay a world of delight.
This was hardly an hour since. Now, like a bubble, abruptly that world had burst.
Yet why?
In that query was the riddle which impotently Verplank was trying to solve. With a clutch at a possible solution, he turned to his servant:
“Roberts, get a motor. If Mrs. Verplank is not on the yacht, I will take a special, and follow her.”
===Chinese===
其间，一些更寻常的事务把弗普兰克从纽波特带到了旧金山（San Francisco）。得知这道禁令——于蕾拉是激励，于他是冒犯——他电报约她到科罗纳多（Coronado）会合，那是南加州的一处度假地，两人本已一起准备离开那里。
前一夜，弗普兰克乘一艘在金门（Golden Gate）包下的游艇到了。第二天早晨，蕾拉是坐火车来的。婚礼随即举行。他们面前铺展着一个欢愉的世界。
这还是不到一个钟头之前的事。此刻，那个世界像个肥皂泡，倏地破了。
可是，为什么？
这个问题里，正是那道弗普兰克正徒劳求解的谜。他猛地抓住一个可能的答案，转向仆人：
“罗伯茨，去叫一辆汽车。要是弗普兰克夫人不在艇上，我就包一列专车，追上去。”

===Original===
“Yes, sir. Shall you wish me to go with you?”
“No, stay here until you hear from me. At any moment Mrs. Verplank may return.”
---
But Leilah did not return. Nor did the special, in which Verplank followed, overtake her. The first intelligence of her that reached him was the announcement of her engagement to another man.
===Chinese===
“是，先生。您要我跟您去吗？”
“不，你留在这儿，等我的消息。弗普兰克夫人随时可能回来。”
---
然而蕾拉没有回来。弗普兰克乘专车追去，也没能追上她。他得到关于她的第一个消息，是她与另一个男人订婚的公告。

## II / 二

===Original===
In Paris, many moons later, an Englishman, Howard Tempest, looked in, at the Opéra, on his cousin, Camille de Joyeuse. This lady, connected by birth with Britannia’s best, and, through her husband, with the Bourbons, delighted the eye, the ear, and the palate. In appearance, she suggested certain designs of Boucher; in colouring and in manner, the Pompadour. Admirable in these respects, she was admired also, for her gayety, her tireless smile, and her chef. She had one of the best cooks in Paris﻿—that is to say, in the world. Her husband, the Duc de Joyeuse, harmonised very perfectly with her. He had a head, empty, but noble, an air vaguely *Régence*. A year younger than herself, Time had had the impertinence to whiten his hair. The duchess was forty-two. Those unaware of the fact fancied her twenty-eight. The error greatly gratified this lady, who, familiarly, was known as Muffins.
One evening in May, Tempest entered her box, saluted her, examined the house, and, as, in a crash of the orchestra, the curtain fell, seated himself, in response to a gesture, beside her.
Camille de Joyeuse turned to him, and with that smile of hers, said: “Do not fail to come on Sunday, Howard. There is to be a Madame Barouffska, whom I want you to meet. She was formerly a Mrs. Verplank. Barouffski is Number Two.”
“Verplank! Barouffski! What barbarous names!” Tempest exclaimed. He had vivid red hair, violent blue eyes, and a great scarlet cicatrix that tore one side of his face. In spite of the severity of his evening clothes, he looked rather barbarous himself. “What was she, a widow?”

===Chinese===
许多个月圆月缺之后，在巴黎，一位名叫霍华德·坦皮斯特（Howard Tempest）的英国人，到歌剧院去探望他的表姐卡米耶·德·茹瓦耶兹（Camille de Joyeuse）。这位女士论血统连着不列颠的顶尖门第，论姻亲又通过丈夫连上了波旁王族，娱人眼目、悦人耳鼓、饱人口福。论容貌，她令人想起布歇（Boucher）的某些图样；论色彩与做派，则是蓬帕杜（the Pompadour）之风。除了这几样可敬之处，她还因她的欢快、她那不知疲倦的微笑和她的厨子而受人仰慕。她拥有全巴黎最好的厨子之一——也就是说，全世界最好的之一。她的丈夫德·茹瓦耶兹公爵与她相配得完美至极。他有一颗头脑，空空如也，却气派高贵，隐隐透着一股*Régence*（法国摄政时期）的风味。他比她小一岁，时间却放肆无礼，把他的头发染白了。公爵夫人四十二岁。不知情的人还当她二十八。这一错觉令这位女士大为受用——熟友们惯常管她叫「玛芬」（Muffins）。
五月的一个夜晚，坦皮斯特走进她的包厢，向她行礼，打量了一番剧场；随后，在一阵乐队的轰鸣声中幕落了下来，他应她一个手势，在她身旁坐下。
卡米耶·德·茹瓦耶兹转过身来，带着她那种微笑说道：「星期天你可一定要来，霍华德。到时有位巴罗夫斯卡夫人（Madame Barouffska），我想让你见见。她从前是位弗普兰克太太（Mrs. Verplank）。巴罗夫斯基（Barouffski）是第二任。」
「弗普兰克！巴罗夫斯基！多野蛮的名字！」坦皮斯特嚷道。他有一头鲜艳的红发、一双暴烈的蓝眼睛，脸的一侧撕着一道大大的猩红色瘢痕。尽管晚礼服严峻得一丝不苟，他本人看上去也相当野蛮。「她当时是寡妇？」

===Original===
“Yes, but with no tombstone to show. It appears that she was in love with Verplank for years, married him one minute and left him the next.”
Tempest stifled a yawn. “How extremely fastidious!”
“She ran away, got a divorce, met Barouffski and married him.”
“Very honourable of her, certainly. From what pond did you fish her?”
“The Silverstairs’. Violet Silverstairs is an American you know﻿—”

===Chinese===
「是的，不过没有墓碑可以出示。据说她爱弗普兰克爱了好些年，嫁给他一分钟，下一分钟就离开了他。」
坦皮斯特忍住一个哈欠。「真是挑剔到家了！」
「她跑掉了，离了婚，遇上巴罗夫斯基，嫁给了他。」
「要说她，那可真体面。你从哪个池塘里钓上她来的？」
「西尔弗斯泰尔斯家。维奥莱特·西尔弗斯泰尔斯（Violet Silverstairs）是美国人，你知道的——」

===Original===
“Know! I should say I did know. Though, if I did not, I would take my oath to it. It’s got so a fellow can’t stir without running into one of them. How does Louis like her?”
Louis was the duke.
The duchess displayed her beautiful false teeth. “Oddly enough, when he was in the States, he went hunting with her Number One.”
“In the Rockies?” Tempest, with sudden interest, inquired. “In the Dakotas?”

===Chinese===
「知道！岂止是知道。就算不知道，我也能对它赌咒发誓。已经到了这步田地：随便谁一动弹，就得撞上他们中的一个。路易觉得她怎么样？」
路易就是那位公爵。
公爵夫人露出她一口漂亮的假牙。「说来也怪，他在美国的时候，还跟她的一号丈夫一起去打过猎。」
「在落基山？」坦皮斯特陡然来了兴致，问道。「在达科他？」

===Original===
“I fancy so. It was a place called, let me see; yes, Long Island, I think. I remember, he said it was very jolly.”
Tempest tossed his red head. “Her Number Two, I suppose, is that chap I have seen at the Little Club. The Lord knows how he got there. He looks like a thimblerigger.”
The duchess raised her opera-glass. “Possibly. Nowadays, so many men do, don’t you think? There is Marie de Fresnoy with the Helley-Quetgens! You will have her next to you on Sunday, Howard. Do not lacerate her tender heart.”
At the suggestion, Tempest made a face. His expression amused Camille de Joyeuse. Indulgently she added: “To make up for it you shall take Madame Barouffska out.”
But now the curtain was rising. The clear brilliance of the house faded into a golden gloom.

===Chinese===
「我猜是吧。那地方叫什么来着；对了，我想是长岛（Long Island）。我记得他说那儿非常快活。」
坦皮斯特甩了甩他的红脑袋。「我想，她的二号丈夫，就是我在小俱乐部（the Little Club）见过的那个家伙。天知道他是怎么混进去的。他看上去像个玩藏豆把戏的骗子。」
公爵夫人举起观剧镜。「也许吧。如今这年头，多少男人都像，你说是不是？瞧，玛丽·德·弗雷努瓦（Marie de Fresnoy）跟埃利-凯特根一家（the Helley-Quetgens）在一起！星期天她会坐在你旁边，霍华德。可别割伤她那颗柔嫩的心。」
听了这话，坦皮斯特做了个鬼脸。他的表情逗乐了卡米耶·德·茹瓦耶兹。她宽容地补了一句：「作为补偿，就由你带巴罗夫斯卡夫人入席吧。」
但这时大幕正在升起。剧场清亮的辉煌渐次隐入一片金色的幽暗。

===Original===
On the Sunday following, when Tempest reached the Cours la Reine, in which his cousin resided, there was a motor before the perron, and from it a woman was alighting. As rhythmically, with a grace that is rare in women who are not ballerines, she mounted the stair, Tempest had a vision of a figure, tall and slight, of a mass of black hair, and of a neck emerging from ermine. In the anteroom above, while a servant took from her her cloak and another received Tempest’s hat and coat, he saw that she was extremely beautiful.
Immediately a footman, throwing open a door, announced: “Madame la comtesse Barouffska!” He added at once: “Lord Howard Tempest!”
In this marriage of their names they entered a drawing room in which were the Joyeuses, the Fresnoys, the Silverstairs; others, also, who momentarily were indistinguishable. The room﻿—large, wide, high-ceiled﻿—was decorated gravely, with infinite taste. Beyond it, a suite of salons extended.
Camille de Joyeuse, advancing to meet her guests, presented Tempest to Mme. Barouffska.

===Chinese===
接下来的那个星期天，坦皮斯特到达他表姐居住的王后大道（Cours la Reine）时，阶前停着一辆汽车，一位女士正从车上下来。她拾级而上，步态富有韵律，有一种非芭蕾舞演员的女子身上罕见的优雅；坦皮斯特瞥见一个颀长纤秀的身影、一头浓密的黑发、一段从貂皮中露出的脖颈。在楼上的前厅里，一个仆人接过她的斗篷，另一个接过坦皮斯特的帽子和外套，他这才看清，她美得出奇。
随即一名男仆推开一扇门，通报道：「巴罗夫斯卡伯爵夫人！」紧接着又报：「霍华德·坦皮斯特勋爵！」
两人的名字就这样被撮合成双，他们随之走进一间客厅，厅内坐着茹瓦耶兹夫妇、弗雷努瓦夫妇、西尔弗斯泰尔斯夫妇，还有另一些人，一时分辨不清。这间屋子——宽大、开阔、顶棚高敞——装饰得庄重而品味无穷。屋子深处，一连几间沙龙次第铺展。
卡米耶·德·茹瓦耶兹迎上前去接待宾客，把坦皮斯特介绍给巴罗夫斯卡夫人。

===Original===
In a voice which, if a trifle high, was fluted, the duchess added:
“My dear, this cousin of mine has a terrible reputation, and that, I am sure, will commend him to you.”
With the semblance of a smile, Mme. Barouffska replied:
“You know I am never quite able to decide just what construction to put on your remarks.”

===Chinese===
公爵夫人用一种嗓音补充道，那嗓音虽略偏高，却如长笛一般：
「亲爱的，我这位表弟名声坏得很，而我敢肯定，这一点恰恰会让他讨你欢心。」
巴罗夫斯卡夫人带着一丝近似微笑的神情答道：
「你知道，你的话我从来拿不准该往哪种意思上解。」

===Original===
“Put the worst, put the worst!” answered the duchess, whose costume left her splendidly nude. From a billowy corsage her shoulders and bust emerged as though rising through foam, while the light gold tissue of her gown accentuated the royal outlines of her figure.
Leilah Barouffska, slenderer, taller, wholly in white, contrasted ethereally with her. Turning to Tempest she said:
“Lord Howard, I have heard so much that is interesting about you.”
“Not from Muffins, then.”

===Chinese===
「往最坏处想，尽管往最坏处想！」公爵夫人答道。她的那身装束让她壮观地赤裸着。从波浪起伏的胸衣里，她的双肩和胸脯涌现出来，仿佛正从泡沫中升起，礼服上轻盈的金色薄纱又勾勒出她身姿雍容华贵的轮廓。
蕾拉·巴罗夫斯卡比她更纤瘦、更高挑，通身上下一片白，与她形成空灵的对照。她转向坦皮斯特，说道：
「霍华德勋爵，我听过许多关于你的趣闻。」
「那总不会是听玛芬说的。」

===Original===
“Yes, but also from Silverstairs. He told me that you are the best gentleman jockey in England and a Sanskrit scholar besides.”
“Oh, I can straddle a horse, if it comes to that, but otherwise he exaggerates. He has caught that from his wife﻿—unless it happens to be from her sister.”
At mention of the girl, Leilah, who had been looking across the room, turned to Tempest again. In looking she saw this young woman whose allurements﻿—and possibilities﻿—were generally regarded as excessive. Recently she had become engaged﻿—perhaps for the tenth time. Coincidentally was the announcement that she was going in for light opera. Now, in reference to her, Leilah said:
“You have met Aurelia, then?”

===Chinese===
「是她说的，不过也有西尔弗斯泰尔斯的份。他告诉我，你是英国最好的绅士骑师，此外还是位梵文学者。」
「哦，真到那个份上，马我倒还跨得上去，别的方面他就是夸大其词了。他这毛病是跟他妻子学的——要不然就是跟她妹妹学的。」
一提到那位姑娘，原本正望着房间另一头的蕾拉又转向坦皮斯特。张望之间，她看见的正是这个年轻女人：她的魅力——以及她的种种可能性——公认过了头。最近她订了婚——也许是第十次了。与订婚巧合的是，同时宣布了她要投身轻歌剧。此刻，谈到她，蕾拉说道：
「那么，你见过奥蕾莉亚（Aurelia）了？」

===Original===
“I found it very difficult not to.”
“And this young Lord Buttercups to whom she is engaged, is he nice?”
Tempest adjusted his monocle. “Very. A trifle wrong in the upper story. So was his father. So was his grandfather. A fine old English family.”
Faintly, as before, Leilah smiled. “I understand that Aurelia is studying for the stage. Such a queer idea, don’t you think﻿—for an American heiress, I mean.”
Tempest, extracting his eyeglass, nodded. “Nowadays, unless an idea is queer, it can hardly be called an idea at all. But I am glad this young person is studying something. When she went to school she must have been taught everything which it is easiest to forget.”

===Chinese===
「我发现想不见她都难。」
「还有她订婚的这位年轻的巴特卡普斯勋爵（Lord Buttercups，意为「毛茛」），人好吗？」
坦皮斯特扶了扶单片眼镜。「非常好。只是楼上那一层有点毛病。他父亲如此。他祖父也如此。一个优秀的英国旧世家。」
蕾拉像刚才一样淡淡一笑。「我听说奥蕾莉亚在学戏。多古怪的念头，你说是不是——我是说，对一个美国女继承人而言。」
坦皮斯特取下夹鼻眼镜，点了点头。「如今这年头，一个念头要是不古怪，几乎就算不得念头。不过我很高兴这位年轻人总算在学点什么。她上学的时候，学到的想必尽是些最容易忘掉的东西。」

===Original===
A servant announced:
“His Excellency, Mustim Pasha!”
The man who entered was short and stout. He had a full black beard, and the appearance, slightly Hebraic, which Turks possess. After M. de Joyeuse had greeted him, he saluted the duchess.
Beyond, on a sofa, Violet Silverstairs sat talking to the Baronne de Fresnoy, a young woman who looked very much as might a statuette of Tanagra, to which Grévin had given two big blue circles for eyes, and a small pink one for mouth, but a statuette articulated, perhaps, by Eros, and costumed, certainly, by the Rue de la Paix﻿—though a shade less artistically than Lady Silverstairs, who always seemed to have just issued from some paradise inhabited solely by poet-modistes, and who, in addition, possessed what Mme. de Fresnoy lacked, a face delicately and rarely patrician.

===Chinese===
一个仆人通报：
「穆斯提姆·帕夏（Mustim Pasha）阁下！」
进来的男人矮而壮实。他蓄着一把浓密的黑胡须，带着土耳其人特有的、微带希伯来气质的相貌。德·茹瓦耶兹先生向他致意之后，他向公爵夫人行了礼。
再远处的一张沙发上，坐着维奥莱特·西尔弗斯泰尔斯，正与弗雷努瓦男爵夫人交谈。这位年轻女子看上去极像一尊塔纳格拉（Tanagra）小陶俑——格雷万（Grévin）给它画上两个大蓝圈作眼睛、一个小粉圈作嘴——只是这尊陶俑的关节或许是厄洛斯（Eros）给装的，衣装则必定出自和平街（Rue de la Paix）之手；虽然比西尔弗斯泰尔斯夫人稍稍少了一分艺术——那位夫人永远像刚从某个只住着诗人女裁缝的乐园里走出来似的，而且，她还拥有德·弗雷努瓦夫人所欠缺的东西：一张精致而罕见地透着贵胄之气的脸。

===Original===
Adjacently was her sister, Aurelia, a girl with a face like an opening rose, and a frock of such astonishing simplicity that it looked both virginal and ruinous. This young person had the loveliest eyes imaginable. In them and about an uncommonly bewitching mouth was an expression quite ideally ingénue which, when least expected, it amused her to transform into one of extreme effrontery.
On one side of her was Lord Buttercups, an English youth, small, snubnosed, stupid. On the other lounged a Roman, Prince Farnese, a remarkably fine-looking pauper.
Turning from the girl’s sister to the Turk, the young baroness called:
“Here, Musty, come and make love to us.”

===Chinese===
她旁边是她妹妹奥蕾莉亚，一个脸如初绽玫瑰的姑娘，一身连衣裙简洁得惊人，看上去既清纯又倾国倾城。这位年轻女子有一双所能想象的最可爱的眼睛。这双眼睛里，连同那张出奇诱人的小嘴四周，总带着一种十足天真烂漫的稚气神情；而在人们最意想不到的时刻，她却乐于把它换成一副极端放肆的神气。
她一侧是巴特卡普斯勋爵，一个矮小、狮子鼻、愚钝的英国青年。另一侧懒懒地倚着一个罗马人，法尔内塞亲王（Prince Farnese），一个相貌极其英俊的穷光蛋。
年轻的男爵夫人从姑娘的姐姐那边转过身来，冲那土耳其人喊道：
「过来，穆斯提，来陪我们说说情话。」

===Original===
The Asiatic was about to abandon Mme. de Joyeuse, when doors at the farther end of the room were thrown open, and the duchess put a hand on his arm.
At table, Tempest, who had taken Leilah Barouffska out, found his seat indicated beside her. At his left was Mme. de Fresnoy, whom he detested. He turned to the American. At the moment some preoccupation, a nostalgia or a regret, contracted the angle of her mouth. The contraction gave her the expression which those display who have deeply suffered either from some long malady or from some perilous constraint.
Mechanically, Tempest considered a dish which a footman, his hands gloved in silk, was presenting. When he again turned to the American, it was as though a curtain had fallen or risen. Her face had lighted, and it was with an entirely worldly air that she put before him this unworldly question:

===Chinese===
这位亚洲人正要离开德·茹瓦耶兹夫人，房间远处的几扇门忽然敞开，公爵夫人把一只手搭上了他的手臂。
席间，搀巴罗夫斯卡夫人入席的坦皮斯特发现，他的座位正安排在她身旁。他左边是德·弗雷努瓦夫人，一个他厌恶的人。他转向这位美国人。此刻某种心事，一缕乡愁或一憾，收紧了她嘴角的线条。这一收紧给了她一种神情——那是久罹沉疴或身陷险境之人脸上才有的神情。
坦皮斯特机械地打量着一道由双手戴着丝手套的男仆端上来的菜。等他再转向这位美国人时，仿佛一道幕落下了、又升起了。她的脸亮了起来，带着一种全然世俗的神气，向他提出了这个非世俗的问题：

===Original===
“Do you believe in fate?”
Tempest laughed. “Not on an empty stomach. I believe then in nothing but virtue.”
Leilah put down her spoon. “It seems to me that our lives are sketched in advance. It may be that we have the power to amplify incidents or to curtail them, but the events themselves remain unchanged. They are there in our paths awaiting us. Though why they are there﻿—”
As was usual with her, she spoke with little pauses, in a voice that caressed the ear. Now she stopped and raised the spoon, in which was almond soup.

===Chinese===
「你相信命运吗？」
坦皮斯特笑了。「空着肚子可不信。那会儿我什么都不信，只信美德。」
蕾拉放下汤匙。「在我看来，我们的一生是预先勾好草稿的。我们也许有权把情节放大或裁短，但事件本身不变。它们就在我们的路上等着我们。至于它们为什么在那儿——」
照她的习惯，她说得顿顿挫挫，嗓音抚着人的耳朵。这时她停下来，举起了汤匙，匙中是杏仁汤。

===Original===
Tempest took a sip of Madeira. “A pal of mine, a chap I never met for a number of reasons, though particularly, I suppose, because he died two thousand years ago, well, he told me that we should wish things to be as they are. I have no quarrel with fate. But if you have, or do have﻿—”
A maître d’hôtel, after presenting a carp that had been arranged as though swimming in saffron, was supervising its service.
“Padapoulos,” exclaimed the young Baronne de Fresnoy, whom the sight of the fish had, perhaps, excited, “Padapoulos told me that he dined best on an orchid soup, a mousse of aubergine, and the maxims of Confucius.”
“Padapoulos,” the legate of the Sublime Porte gravely commented, “is a poet, and a Greek. Add those two things together, and you get﻿—you get﻿—”

===Chinese===
坦皮斯特呷了一口马德拉酒。「我有个朋友，一个我从没见过的人——原因有很多，不过主要的一条，我猜，是他在两千年前就死了——嗯，他告诉我，我们应当愿万事万物如其所是。我对命运没有怨言。不过你若有怨，或者确有——」
一位司膳总管刚呈上一尾鲤鱼——被摆布得仿佛正游弋在番红花里——此刻正督着人分菜。
「帕达普洛斯（Padapoulos），」年轻的弗雷努瓦男爵夫人嚷道，那尾鱼的模样或许让她兴奋了，「帕达普洛斯跟我说过，他吃得最香的一顿，是兰花汤、茄子慕斯，外加孔子的格言。」
「帕达普洛斯，」奥斯曼朝廷的这位使节庄严评论道，「是个诗人，又是个希腊人。把这两样加在一起，你就得到——你就得到——」

===Original===
“Nothing to eat!” the young baroness, with an explosion of little laughs, threw at him. “Musty!” she cried. “Whom were you with at the Variétés last night? I saw you. Yes, I did. Oh, Musty, who would have thought that you would be unfaithful to me!”
“These Roumis!” the Turk mentally exclaimed. “If a wife of mine talked in that way I would have her impaled.”
Beyond, across an opulent bosom, de Joyeuse and Silverstairs were talking sport. They delighted in things that men have always loved, the pursuit of prey, the joy of killing, the murderous serenity of the woods.
Farther up was Aurelia. As before, she had Buttercups on one side, Farnese on the other. She poked at the former.

===Chinese===
「没东西可吃！」年轻男爵夫人一阵小小的爆笑，劈头掷给他。「穆斯提！」她喊道。「昨晚在杂耍剧院（the Variétés）你跟谁在一起？我看见你了。对，就是我。噢，穆斯提，谁能想到你竟会对我不忠！」
「这些鲁米人（Roumis，土耳其人对西方基督徒的称呼）！」土耳其人心里暗叫。「我若有个老婆敢这样讲话，我就叫人把她钉上尖桩。」
再过去，隔着一道丰腴的胸脯，德·茹瓦耶兹和西尔弗斯泰尔斯正谈着运动。他们喜爱男人一贯热爱的那些东西：对猎物的追捕、杀戮的快感、林间那杀气腾腾的宁静。
更远处是奥蕾莉亚。和先前一样，她一边是巴特卡普斯，一边是法尔内塞。她捅了捅前者。

===Original===
“That horrid de Fresnoy woman is trying to flirt with you, Parsnips. Now, don’t deny it. I don’t blame her. You are too good-looking. When we are married﻿—if we ever are﻿—I’ll make you wear a veil. You shan’t go out except in a closed carriage. Yes, and with some big, fat, strapping woman to look after you.”
Beatifically the youth considered her. “Couldn’t you do that?”
Delicately Aurelia raised a fork. “I shall have my own affairs to attend to.” For a second she nibbled. “I have a few on my hands as it is.”
Buttercups stabbed at his plate. “I, too, may have business of my own.”

===Chinese===
「那个讨厌的德·弗雷努婆娘在跟你调情呢，欧防风（Parsnips，意为「欧防风」）。别不承认。我不怪她。你长得太俊了。等我们结了婚——要是真结得成的话——我要你戴上面纱。除了封闭的马车，你哪儿也不许去。对，还得派个高大肥胖、身强力壮的女人看着你。」
那青年一脸福相地端详她。「这件事你来干不行吗？」
奥蕾莉亚优雅地举起叉子。「我自有我的事要操心。」她小口啃了一下。「眼下手里就已经有几桩了。」
巴特卡普斯戳着自己的盘子。「我，说不定也有自己的生意。」

===Original===
“Business! Business!” The girl repeated. “You are so commercial just like all the nobility. If you were not a peer you would be mistaken for one. It’s quite painful.”
“That may b-be,” Buttercups spluttered. “But this idea of yours of going on the stage is quite as p-painful to me.”
He hesitated, then, as though uttering a great moral truth, threw out:
“It’s so dreadful to have your name in the papers!”
“And still more dreadful not to!” the girl threw back. She turned to Farnese. “You do nothing but eat!”

===Chinese===
「生意！生意！」姑娘重复道。「你可真商业化，跟整个贵族阶层一个样。你要不是贵族，准会被人当成贵族。真叫人痛心。」
「也许——也许是吧，」巴特卡普斯口齿不清地说。「可你想登台演戏的这个念头，对我来——来说也一样痛心。」
他迟疑了一下，随后，像宣布一条重大的道德真理似的，抛出一句：
「名字登在报纸上，多可怕呀！」
「不登才更可怕呢！」姑娘回敬道。她转向法尔内塞。「你就知道吃！」

===Original===
With large, melancholy, inconstant eyes the Italian looked at her. “It is my one consolation since you became engaged to that imbecile.”
Aurelia pecked at her food. “One always feels quite safe with an imbecile, and that is so restful. Try it and see.”
“I’d like to try it with you.”
Aurelia put down her fork. “Am I your idea of an imbecile? You are flattering, if insincere.”

===Chinese===
意大利人用那双大大的、忧郁而游移不定的眼睛望着她。「自从你跟那个白痴订了婚，这就是我唯一的安慰了。」
奥蕾莉亚小口啄着食物。「跟白痴在一起总觉得安稳，这多省心。你试试看就知道。」
「我倒想跟你试试。」
奥蕾莉亚放下叉子。「我在你眼里就是白痴？你这是奉承，尽管不真诚。」

===Original===
Again the Roman covered her with his eyes. “It is rather difficult to be the one and not the other. But I am perfectly sincere in saying that you are my idea of perfection.”
“Thanks, but, then, you are not mine.”
“And might one ask what yours is?”

===Chinese===
罗马人又用目光把她罩住。「既要显得真诚又不能不算奉承，两头兼顾可不容易。不过我万分真诚地说：你就是我心中完美的样板。」
「多谢。可惜你可不是我的样板。」
「那能问问你的样板是什么吗？」

===Original===
Dreamily, with an air of innocence that was infinite, Aurelia looked at him. “You will think it childish of me, perhaps, but, like all young girls﻿—like all young and inexperienced girls﻿—I have an ideal. A mere maiden’s fancy, no doubt, and yet one which I cherish so. It is a vision which at times I have of a blind man, a deaf mute, a divine creature, invalid and octogenarian, who would not know what I did and would not care.”
Pausing, she dropped her eyes and sadly shook her head. “You will tell me that he don’t exist.”
“He might be manufactured,” Farnese cheerfully replied.
Then he, too, paused, drank of the wine before him, and, perhaps stimulated by it, whispered:

===Chinese===
恍恍惚惚地，带着一脸无限的纯真，奥蕾莉亚望着他。「你也许会觉得我幼稚，不过，像所有年轻姑娘——像所有年轻而无经验的姑娘一样——我有一个理想。不过是个少女的痴念罢了，可我偏偏珍爱得很。那是一种时而浮现眼前的幻象：一个盲人，一个聋哑人，一个神圣的造物，缠绵病榻的八旬老翁，他不知道我做了什么，也不在乎。」
说罢她垂下眼睛，忧伤地摇了摇头。「你要告诉我，这样的人并不存在。」
「他可以定做嘛，」法尔内塞快快活活地答道。
随后他也顿住了，喝了口面前的酒，也许是酒壮的胆，他低声道：

===Original===
“Believe me, I feel as though I could cut my throat for you.”
Maliciously Aurelia looked up. “When a man does not feel that way he has no feeling at all. One might even say that he is quite heartless.”
Across the table, Tempest, turning again to Leilah, said: “Monsieur Barouffski is not here tonight.”
At the remark, instantly in her face its former expression of constraint appeared.

===Chinese===
「相信我，我觉得自己简直能为你割喉。」
奥蕾莉亚满怀恶意地抬起眼。「男人要是没有这种感觉，那就根本毫无感觉。甚至可以说，全无心肝。」
桌子对面，坦皮斯特又转向蕾拉，说：「巴罗夫斯基先生今晚不在。」
这话一出口，她脸上先前那种紧绷的神情立刻又出现了。

===Original===
“No, he is to join us later.”
At the other end, where the duchess sat, everybody was laughing. The lady had been giving an account of a recent bankruptcy, that of Lord Auld Reekie.
“Heavens!” Violet Silverstairs exclaimed. “What will become of Bobbles!”
Bobbles was Lady Auld Reekie, a fair young craft of light timber and many sails.

===Chinese===
「是的，他晚些会来跟我们会合。」
在桌子另一端公爵夫人坐的地方，人人都笑了起来。那位女士正讲着一桩近来的破产案——奥德·里基勋爵（Lord Auld Reekie）的破产。
「天哪！」维奥莱特·西尔弗斯泰尔斯惊呼。「波布尔斯（Bobbles）可怎么办！」
波布尔斯就是奥德·里基夫人，一艘轻木打造、帆篷层层的年轻漂亮快艇。

===Original===
Camille de Joyeuse, summoning her diligent smile, replied: “She will go into the hands of a receiver.”
Another jest followed, and presently, in the contagion of it almost the entire table joined.
The delicately toxic fare, the slightly emotionalising wines, loosened tongues, robbing them of discretion, and, before the servants, as though the latter were deaf and dumb, hosts and guests revealed their naked minds.
“It is rotten to talk in that way before these men,” Tempest exclaimed. “They get their wages with lessons in anarchy thrown in. It’s too much.”

===Chinese===
卡米耶·德·茹瓦耶兹唤起她那勤勉的微笑，答道：「她会落到破产接管人的手里去。」
接着又是一个笑话，不一会儿，在这笑的传染中，几乎全桌都加入了进来。
精致有毒的菜肴、微微煽情的酒，松了人们的舌头，夺走了它们的分寸；在仆人们面前——仿佛后者又聋又哑——主人和宾客们敞露出赤裸裸的心思。
「当着这些人的面这么讲话，太糟糕了，」坦皮斯特嚷道。「他们领着工钱，还附送无政府主义的课程。太过分了。」

===Original===
“I had not heard,” Leilah replied. “I was thinking of that friend of yours whom you never met.”
Tempest laughed. “The one who said we should wish things to be as they are? Ah, well! I am afraid I am not up to that yet.”
“Nor I. But who was he, if one may ask? Not Aristotle?”
“No, but, by the way, do you know whom Aristotle is supposed to be, or rather to have become? Herbert Spencer! An occultist told me. He told me also such a curious story. You have heard, have you not, of Apollonius of Tyana? Then you may remember it is said of him that he healed the sick, raised the dead, knew all things save the caresses of women, and spoke every language including that of colours. Well, the occultist told me that Jesus was a rabbi who surrendered his entity to the Christ, and afterward reappeared here as Apollonius. He said, too, that he was not crucified. Crucifixion, you know, is merely the symbol of initiation.”

===Chinese===
「我可没听见，」蕾拉答道。「我正在想你那位从未谋面的朋友。」
坦皮斯特笑了。「那个说我们应当愿万事如其所是的人？唉，是啊！恐怕我还做不到。」
「我也做不到。不过能问问他是谁吗？总不会是亚里士多德吧？」
「不是。不过，说到这个，你知道人家认为亚里士多德是谁——或者说变成了谁吗？赫伯特·斯宾塞（Herbert Spencer）！有位通灵术士告诉我的。他还给我讲了个离奇的故事。你总听说过提亚纳的阿波罗尼乌斯（Apollonius of Tyana）吧？那你也许记得，据说他能治病、能起死回生、知晓万事，只不识女人的温存，还会说每一种语言，包括色彩的语言。嗯，那位通灵术士告诉我，耶稣是一位拉比，他把自己的实体交托给了基督，之后又以阿波罗尼乌斯之身重现于世。他还说，耶稣并没有被钉上十字架。你知道，钉十字架只不过是入门的象征。」

===Original===
“And whom did he say was the Christ?”
“An envoy from a higher sphere.”
Leilah inclined her head. “Yes, and there have, I believe, been others. From zeniths or from nadirs unknown to us, from planes, let us say, where all beatitudes are as usual as all shames are common here, spirits commissioned to regenerate the hearts of man pass into the slums of space. Confident, with a crown of light they come, only to return with one of thorns.”
Tempest turned squarely in his chair. “That is a singularly beautiful idea!”

===Chinese===
「那他说基督是谁？」
「一位来自更高境界的使者。」
蕾拉垂了垂首。「是的，而且我相信，此外还有别的。从我们不知的天顶或天底，从那样一些层面——姑且说，在那里一切至福如常，一如这里一切羞耻寻常——奉命来再造人心的精灵，进入太空的贫民窟。他们满怀信心，戴着光之冠冕而来，却戴着荆冠而归。」
坦皮斯特在椅子里正过身来。「这想法美得出奇！」

===Original===
Again Leilah inclined her head. “It is beautiful. It is beautiful to think that earthward from some chromatic star the soul of Krishna may have sunk. But the idea is not mine. I found it in the Vidya.”
This last statement was lost. Mme. de Fresnoy was insisting on Tempest’s attention. Meanwhile a cygnet, its plumage replaced, a pond lily in its ochre beak, had been presented, carved and served. A salad, known as Half-Mourning, a composition of artichoke hearts and Piedmontese truffles, had departed with it. Now sweets had come, pastry light as a caress, volatile as an essence, that pastry of which the art is known only to the Oriental and the occasional cordon bleu.
Devoutly, with an air of invoking the Prophet, the Turk was absorbing it.
The young Baronne de Fresnoy, abandoning Tempest, looked at him. With a wicked glitter in her big blue eyes she called:

===Chinese===
蕾拉又垂了垂首。「是美。想到克里希纳（Krishna）的灵魂可能从某颗彩色星辰向地球沉落，这很美。但这想法不是我的。我是在 the Vidya（梵语「明/智」）里读到的。」
这后一句话被淹没了。德·弗雷努瓦夫人正硬要坦皮斯特听她讲话。与此同时，一只小天鹅——周身羽毛换饰一新、赭色喙间衔一朵睡莲——被呈上、片好、分派。一道名为「半丧服」（Half-Mourning）的沙拉——洋蓟心与皮埃蒙特松露的拼合——随之一同撤下。此刻甜品上来了，那糕点轻盈如一记爱抚，飘忽如一缕香精，那门手艺唯有东方人和偶尔一见的蓝带名厨（cordon bleu）才谙熟。
那位土耳其人一脸虔诚，一副向先知祷祝的神气，正把它徐徐吸纳下肚。
年轻的弗雷努瓦男爵夫人撇下坦皮斯特，望着他。她蓝色的大眼睛里闪着狡黠的光，喊道：

===Original===
“Musty! Are you thinking of me?”
The pasha was framing a reply, a reply perhaps rather bald, when Camille de Joyeuse also addressed him. Presently she stood up. The others imitated her. The gayeties of the table were abandoned for the brilliance of the salons beyond.
Tempest, who had accompanied Leilah Barouffska said, as she seated herself:
“Are you to remain in Paris?”
Before answering, she looked up at him, for he was standing. “Who can tell what one will do? But I fancy so. We have taken a house in the Rue de la Pompe.”

===Chinese===
「穆斯提！你在想我吗？」
帕夏正在斟酌一个回答——一个也许相当露骨的回答——这时卡米耶·德·茹瓦耶兹也对他讲起话来。不一会儿她站起身，众人随之起立。餐桌上的欢宴让位给了外面客厅的辉煌。
陪着蕾拉·巴罗夫斯卡的坦皮斯特，在她落座时说道：
「你们要留在巴黎吗？」
回答之前，她抬头看了看他，因为他还站着。「谁能说准自己将来会做什么呢？不过我想是吧。我们在蓬佩街（Rue de la Pompe）租了一栋房子。」

===Original===
“In the Rue de la Pompe!” Tempest exclaimed. “That is where I live.” He smiled. The fact that they were neighbours seemed to constitute a bond. “Whereabouts in the Rue de la Pompe?”
“Next to the church.”
“Do you find it convenient?”
A servant announced:

===Chinese===
「在蓬佩街！」坦皮斯特失声道。「我就住在那儿。」他微微一笑。做街坊这件事似乎构成了一条纽带。「蓬佩街的哪一段？」
「教堂隔壁。」
「你觉得那儿方便吗？」
一个仆人通报：

===Original===
“Monsieur and madame Spencer-Poole!”
“You mean,” Leilah replied, “am I a Catholic? No, I am an Episcopalian. But my views, I fear, are not orthodox. I have got so far that I believe fully in the Vidya.”
She had cited the book at the dinner table but, at the time, distracted by Marie de Fresnoy, Tempest had not heard; now he exclaimed at it.
“The Vidya! Of all things! Why not the Upanishads?”

===Chinese===
「斯宾塞-普尔先生和夫人（Spencer-Poole）！」
「你是说，」蕾拉答道，「我是不是天主教徒？不是，我是圣公会教徒。不过我的见解，恐怕并不正统。我已经走到这一步：完全信 the Vidya 了。」
晚餐桌上她引过这本书，但当时坦皮斯特被玛丽·德·弗雷努瓦分了神，没有听见；此刻他一听便叫了起来。
「the Vidya！什么不好是！怎么不是《奥义书》（the Upanishads）？」

===Original===
A servant announced:
“Madame la marquise de Charleroi!”
Leilah made a little gesture. “The Upanishads, too. I have great faith in them also. Their conceptions seem to me the most perfect that the human mind has evolved, that is, if it were a human mind that evolved them.”
A servant announced:
“Madame la princesse Orlonna!”

===Chinese===
一个仆人通报：
「夏勒罗伊侯爵夫人（de Charleroi）！」
蕾拉做了个小手势。「《奥义书》也信。我对它们也有极大的信心。在我看来，它们的构想是人心演化出的最完美的东西——如果演化它们的果真是人心的话。」
一个仆人通报：
「奥隆娜王妃（Orlonna）！」

===Original===
“What particularly impressed you in them?” Tempest asked.
“The demonstration that life is a laboratory in which the strength of the soul is tried.”
“And in the Vidya?”
“The fact that selfishness is the root of evil. That impressed me very much, primarily I suppose because it is true, but chiefly I think because I had not realized it before.”
Tempest nodded. Never had he heard a mondaine cite the Upanishads. In no drawing room had he ever heard the Vidya mentioned. In his life he had not dreamed of having a digest of each produced in an atmosphere dripping with frivolities. As he nodded he reconsidered this woman. From the first he had realized that she differed from the ordinary society type. Now he saw that she belonged to a superior world.

===Chinese===
「其中什么特别打动了你？」坦皮斯特问。
「那个论证：生命是一间实验室，灵魂的力量在其中受试。」
「那 the Vidya 里呢？」
「自私是万恶之根这一事实。它深深打动了我，首先我想是因为它是真的，而主要我想是因为我从前没有意识到它。」
坦皮斯特点头。他从没听过一位交际花引证《奥义书》。在任何一间客厅里，他从未听人提起 the Vidya。他平生做梦也想不到，会有人在一间轻浮得直往下滴的空气里，向他端出这两部书各自的摘要。他一边点头，一边重新打量这位女子。从最初起他就意识到，她不同于寻常的社交类型。现在他看出，她属于一个更高的世界。

===Original===
“Do you not admire them, too?” Leilah, who had also been considering him, inquired.
Tempest adjusted his monocle. “You see, you know, the Self, the All-Self, the One, the oneness of self with everything, the oneness of all things with One, these minor motifs of theirs I may admire but I do not grasp. On the contrary, there is a certain voluminous complexity about them that makes me gasp. None the less they advance certain ideas which, while curious to the few and to the many absurd, are yet so mathematically evident; the fact for instance﻿—”
A servant announced:
“His Highness monseigneur le prince Paul de Montebianco!”

===Chinese===
「你不也钦佩它们吗？」也在打量他的蕾拉问道。
坦皮斯特扶了扶单片眼镜。「你瞧，那个自我（the Self）、大全自我（the All-Self）、太一（the One）、自我与万物的合一、万物与太一的合一——他们这些次要的母题，我可以钦佩，却领会不了。相反，它们有一种庞大驳杂的东西，让我喘不上气。尽管如此，它们提出某些观念，虽对少数人显得古怪、对多数人显得荒谬，却又如此数学般地明白；比如那个事实——」
一个仆人通报：
「蒙特比安科亲王保罗殿下（Paul de Montebianco）！」

===Original===
“Monsieur Harris!”
The salons were becoming filled. The floor was swept by trains brief but brilliant. There was a multiplication of black coats, a renewed animation, a mounting murmur in which occasionally the name of a new arrival was lost.
The servant announced:
“Monsieur le vicomte and madame la vicomtesse de Helley-Quetgen!”
“Madame la princesse Zubaroff!”

===Chinese===
「哈里斯先生（Harris）！」
沙龙渐渐坐满。裙裾短促而灿烂，扫过地板。黑礼服成倍增多，一阵新起的活跃，一阵渐高的嗡嗡声，其中不时淹没掉某位新客的名字。
仆人通报：
「埃利-凯特根子爵先生暨子爵夫人！」
「祖巴罗夫王妃（Zubaroff）！」

===Original===
“Monsieur d’Arcy!”
“Monsieur le comte Barouffski!”
The last of these, a large man, very fair, with grey-green eyes, had a studied manner which, however, his voice relieved. As he advanced and addressed Mme. de Joyeuse, it sounded supple and silken, as indeed most Slav voices do.
Already groups had formed. The corner in which Tempest stood before Leilah developed another. The Spencer-Pooles approached. With them was d’Arcy, a young man abominably good looking, famous for the prodigious variety of his affairs.

===Chinese===
「达尔西先生（d’Arcy）！」
「巴罗夫斯基伯爵先生！」
这最后一位是个高大的男人，非常白皙，灰绿色的眼睛，一副刻意雕琢的仪态，不过他的嗓音化解了这种刻意。当他走上前去向德·茹瓦耶兹夫人致意时，那嗓音响起来柔韧如丝，正如大多数斯拉夫嗓音一样。
人群早已围成一簇簇。坦皮斯特站在蕾拉面前的那一角又聚起一簇。斯宾塞-普尔夫妇走近来。与他们同来的还有达尔西，一个俊美得可憎的年轻人，以其情事之纷繁多样而闻名。

===Original===
Tempest who had continued talking, who had even been expounding and who now felt that he had been holding forth, moved on. He wanted to smoke and being an habitué of the household, he knew where the smoking room was.
There, before an open fire, his hands behind his back, in that after-dinner attitude which some men assume, M. de Joyeuse stood. He was telling of a stag hunt that had been held at Monplaisir, his estate.
The duke was not an impressionist, his description lacked colour. But de Fresnoy, who had been present, resaw it all; the sheen of the horses, the green of the whippers-in, the pink coats of the sportsmen, the blue dolmans of the officers that had ridden over from a garrison nearby, the verdure of the forest’s edge, the view, the scramble, the run, the quarry, the hallali of the huntsman, the leaping hounds, the fastidious ceremonial of the death and the sky of pale silk which draped with faint gold the magnificent brutality of the scene.

===Chinese===
坦皮斯特一直在讲，甚至一直在阐发，此刻觉得自己分明是在高谈阔论了，便挪开了。他想抽烟，而作为这家的常客，他知道吸烟室在哪儿。
那儿，壁炉前，双手背在身后，摆着某些男人饭后惯有姿势站着的，是德·茹瓦耶兹先生。他正讲着一场猎鹿，那是在他名为蒙普莱西尔（Monplaisir）的庄园里举行的。
公爵不是印象派，他的描述缺乏色彩。但在场的德·弗雷努瓦却在心里把一切重看了一遍：马匹的光泽、引猎仆从的一身绿、猎手的粉红外套、从附近驻地骑马赶来的军官们的蓝色多曼短袄、林缘的苍翠、望见猎物、乱纷纷的争夺、一路的追奔、被逐的猎物、猎人吹响的围猎号角、腾跃的猎犬、死亡那套考究的仪礼，还有一片淡丝绸色的天空，以一抹微金，给这幅堂堂的残暴披上帷幕。

===Original===
“It was just my luck to have missed it,” Silverstairs threw in.
De Joyeuse turned to him. “We count on you next autumn. And on you also, *mon vieux*,” he added to Tempest who had approached.
Tempest nodded. He was lighting a cigar. The operation concluded, he drew a chair beside Silverstairs. “Now, tell me all about Madame B.”
Silverstairs eyed him quizzingly. “She interests you?”

===Chinese===
「偏偏我没赶上，真是我的运气，」西尔弗斯泰尔斯插嘴道。
德·茹瓦耶兹转向他。「明年秋天我们指望你。还有你，*mon vieux*（老伙计），」他对走过来的坦皮斯特补了一句。
坦皮斯特点点头。他正点着一支雪茄。点完了，他在西尔弗斯泰尔斯身旁拉过一把椅子。「好了，把 B 夫人（Madame B.）的事从头讲给我听吧。」
西尔弗斯泰尔斯打趣地瞧着他。「她让你感兴趣？」

===Original===
“Enormously.”
“Then look out for Barouffski whom she interests still more.”
Tempest shrugged his shoulders. “Was it her interest in Number One or Number One’s interest in her that declined?”
“You mean Verplank?”

===Chinese===
「兴趣大极了。」
「那你就当心巴罗夫斯基吧，她让他兴趣更大。」
坦皮斯特耸耸肩。「是她对一号的兴趣减退了呢，还是一号对她的兴趣减退了？」
「你是说弗普兰克？」

===Original===
“I suppose I do. Anyway I mean her first husband. Why were they divorced?”
“Why? But my dear Tempest, divorce in the States is what racing is with us, a national amusement. Everybody takes a hand in it.”
“The right or the left?”
“Both I fancy. Though in the case of Madame B. I have an idea that the right turned out to be wrong.”

===Chinese===
「大概吧。反正我是说她第一任丈夫。他们为什么离婚？」
「为什么？嗳，亲爱的坦皮斯特，在美国，离婚就像我们这儿的赛马，是一项全民消遣。人人都插上一手。」
「用右手还是左手？」
「我想两只手都用。不过就 B 夫人这桩案子而言，我有个想法：那只右手到头来偏偏是错的。」

===Original===
Tempest flicked the ashes from his cigar. “I may compliment you, Silverstairs. You have a manner of expressing yourself which is highly cryptic. But now, to an everyday sort of chap like myself, would you mind being less abstruse?”
“I should feel sordid if I refused. Verplank is a very good sort, whereas this Barouffski is a rotter.”
Tempest bowed. “Thank you for descending to my level. The long and short of it is that she has made a mess of it. Well, most people do. I don’t wonder now that over the soup she talked about fate.”
“Oh, as for that, after certain experiences of my own, with which, pray do not be alarmed, I have no intention of boring you, I have stopped wondering at anything at all.”

===Chinese===
坦皮斯特弹了弹雪茄灰。「我可以恭维你一句，西尔弗斯泰尔斯。你有一种自我表达的功夫，深奥得很。不过现在，对我这样一个平平常常的人，你可否少些玄虚？」
「拒绝的话，我就显得庸俗了。弗普兰克是头等的好人，而这位巴罗夫斯基是个无赖。」
坦皮斯特一躬身。「谢谢你屈尊降到我的层次。长话短说：她把事情搞砸了。唉，多数人都这样。难怪她喝汤的时候大谈命运。」
「哦，那个嘛，自从我自己有了某些经历之后——请别惊慌，我无意拿它们来烦你——我已经对任何事都不再惊奇了。」

===Original===
“Silverstairs, in ceasing to be cryptic, do not become Spartan. My cousin told me that Joyeuse hunted with this, with What’s-his-name, with﻿—er﻿—”
“With Verplank?”
“Yes, that he had hunted with him in the States. And that reminds me. What have you decided about that horse?”
Silverstairs pulled at his straw-coloured moustache. “I’ll let you know tomorrow. When are you to be at home?”

===Chinese===
「西尔弗斯泰尔斯，别再玄虚了，可也别变得斯巴达。我表姐告诉我，茹瓦耶兹跟这个——跟那谁——跟呃——一起打过猎。」
「跟弗普兰克？」
「对，说他在美国跟他一起打过猎。这倒提醒我了。那匹马你拿定主意了吗？」
西尔弗斯泰尔斯捻着他的草黄色胡须。「明天给你答复。你什么时候在家？」

===Original===
“Any time after two.”
Silverstairs nodded. “Very good, I will drop in on you.”
From beyond, blue and vibrant, came the upper notes of a violin. In the now crowded salons a Romanian, the rage of the season, a youth, very pale, with melancholy eyes, flowing hair and the waist of a girl, was executing a fantasy of his own.
De Joyeuse flicked a speck from his sleeve, threw back his noble and empty head, gave a circular look of inquiry, a little gesture of invitation, and accompanied by his friends, sauntered to the rooms without.

===Chinese===
「两点以后，任何时候。」
西尔弗斯泰尔斯点点头。「很好，我去你那儿坐坐。」
从远处传来小提琴的高音，湛蓝而颤动。此刻，在拥挤的沙龙里，一位罗马尼亚人——本季红得发紫的人物，一个脸色极白、眼睛忧郁、长发披垂、腰身纤细如少女的青年——正在演奏一首他自己的幻想曲。
德·茹瓦耶兹从袖上弹掉一粒微尘，把他那颗高贵而空空的脑袋向后一仰，环视一圈以示询问，又做了一个邀请的小手势，便由朋友们陪着，信步走向外面各厅。

===Original===
There, Barouffski after saluting Mme. de Joyeuse had engaged her briefly in talk. But her attention had been attracted rather than claimed by the Montebiancan prince, a young man extremely gentlemanly and equally modest who, with that diffidence which royals and poets share, stood bashfully at her side.
Barouffski, bowing again, passed on. During his short and entirely fragmentary conversation with Mme. de Joyeuse, his eyes had rummaged the room.
Leilah, meanwhile, rising from the sofa where she had been seated, moved with the inflammatory d’Arcy into the salon beyond.
Barouffski would have followed. But the young Baronne de Fresnoy addressed him. Perversely, with sudden glimpses of little teeth and an expression of glee in her piquant face, she asked:

===Chinese===
那边，巴罗夫斯基在向德·茹瓦耶兹夫人行礼之后，同她简短交谈了几句。但她的注意力与其说是被抓住了，不如说是被那位蒙特比安科的亲王吸引了——一个极其绅士而又同样谦逊的年轻人，带着王室与诗人共有的那种羞怯，讪讪地站在她身旁。
巴罗夫斯基再次一躬，走了过去。在与德·茹瓦耶兹夫人那段简短而全然支离破碎的谈话期间，他的眼睛早把整个房间翻检了一遍。
与此同时，蕾拉从她坐着的沙发上站起身，挽着那个惹是生非的达尔西，走进了里面的沙龙。
巴罗夫斯基本想跟过去。但年轻的弗雷努瓦男爵夫人叫住了他。她促狭地、忽然露出一口小牙、俏丽的脸上带着欢欣的神情，问道：

===Original===
“Was it you who performed that high act of gallantry at Longchamps today?”
“Was it I who did what?” Barouffski surprisedly exclaimed.
“What was it?” asked Aurelia, who with Buttercups in tow, had approached.
But Mme. de Fresnoy waved at her. “Go away my dear, it is not for an ingénue.”

===Chinese===
「今天在隆尚（Longchamps）演出那幕骑士义举的，是你吗？」
「是我干了什么？」巴罗夫斯基愕然叫道。
「什么事？」奥蕾莉亚问，她拖着巴特卡普斯，也凑了过来。
但德·弗雷努瓦夫人朝她摆摆手。「走开，亲爱的，这不是天真少女听的东西。」

===Original===
“Ah then, but you see,” Aurelia indolently interjected, “I am tired of being an ingénue. An ingénue is supposed to be in a state of constant surprise and that is so exhausting.”
None the less, with Buttercups still in tow she betook herself to a corner where she was promptly joined by Farnese.
Then at once to Barouffski, to Mustim Pasha, to the Helley-Quetgens, to others that stood about, the young baroness related a morsel of gossip, the report of which had been brought her but a moment before, a story that had one of the reigning demireps for heroine and for hero a man unidentified by the baronne’s informant, the tale of an assault committed before all Paris, before all Paris that is, that happened to be at the races that day; an extravaganza in which the heroine, erupting suddenly on the *pelouse* before the Grand Stand, had, with her parasol, struck the hero over the head and had been about to strike him again, when he, pinioning her arms with his own, had to the applause of everybody, prevented the second assault by kissing her through her veil; after which releasing the lady, he had raised his hat and strolled away.

===Chinese===
「啊，可是你要知道，」奥蕾莉亚懒懒地插嘴，「天真少女我当腻了。天真少女理应处在不停的惊奇之中，那太累人了。」
尽管如此，她仍拖着巴特卡普斯，挪到一个角落里，法尔内塞随即也跟了过去。
于是，年轻的男爵夫人立刻向巴罗夫斯基、向穆斯提姆帕夏、向埃利-凯特根一家、向站在近旁的其他人，讲起一小段闲话，一则片刻之前才有人捎给她的传闻：故事的女主角是当红的某位高等交际花，男主角据报信人所说则是个无名氏；一桩当着全巴黎——准确地说，当着那天碰巧在赛马场的全巴黎——犯下的暴行；一出趣剧：女主角突然冲上大看台前的*pelouse*（内场草坪），用阳伞往男主角头上敲了一记，正要敲第二记时，男主角伸出双臂钳住她的双臂，在满场喝彩声中，隔着面纱吻了她，就此防止了第二记殴打；随后他放开那位女士，举帽为礼，扬长而去。

===Original===
“Was it you, Barouffski?” Mme. de Fresnoy, the narrative at an end, inquired. “Was it?”
“I? Nonsense! Why should you ask?”
“It would be just like you, you know. Besides, I hear that the man was tall and good-looking.”
“You are exceedingly complimentary. But the world is peopled with tall, good-looking men.”

===Chinese===
故事讲完，德·弗雷努瓦夫人问道：「是你吗，巴罗夫斯基？是不是？」
「我？胡说！你为什么这么问？」
「你知道，这可真像你的作风。再说，我听说那人又高又俊。」
「你真是过奖了。可世上多的是又高又俊的男人。」

===Original===
“*Pas tant que ça*,” laughed the baroness. “Well, if it was not you, perhaps it was that man who is just coming in.”
Involuntarily Barouffski turned, while a footman bawled:
“Monsieur Verplank!”

===Chinese===
「*Pas tant que ça*（可没那么多），」男爵夫人笑道。「好吧，如果不是你，也许是正进门的那位。」
巴罗夫斯基本能地转过身去，一个男仆正扯着嗓子高声通报：
「弗普兰克先生！」

## III / 三

===Original===
It was in circumstances which, if not dramatic, were, at least, uncommon, that Leilah Verplank met Barouffski.

At Los Angeles, after her flight from Coronado, she caught an express that would have taken her East. Even so, it could not take her from herself, it could not distance memory, it could not annihilate the past. The consciousness of that obsessed her. Each of her thoughts became a separate throb. About her head formed an iron band. Her body ached. She felt hot and ill. She had a sense of thirst, a sense, too, of fear.

In the compartment where she sat, a stranger came. She hid her face, covering it with her hands. The stranger sidled in between them, looked her in the eyes, penetrated them, permeated her, shook long shudders through her, shrieked at her: “I am Fright!”

She cried aloud. No one heard. She got to the door.

In the section immediately adjoining were her women. Perplexed at the start by her unaccountable flight and, since then, alarmed by the abnormal excitability which she had displayed, both, at the sight of her then, rushed to her.
===Chinese===
蕾拉·弗普兰克与巴罗夫斯基相遇的情境，纵然算不上戏剧性，至少也属罕有。

在洛杉矶，逃离科罗纳多之后，她登上一列本可载她东去的快车。然而即便如此，那列车带不走她自己，拉不开记忆的距离，也无法将往昔一笔勾销。这样的意识纠缠着她。她的每一个念头都变成一次独立的抽痛。她的头上箍起了一道铁箍。她周身作痛，只觉燥热难当、浑身不适。她感到口渴，也感到恐惧。

在她独坐的车厢隔间里，来了一个陌生人。她掩起面孔，双手遮脸。那陌生人从她指掌之间侧身挤了进来，直盯她的眼睛，看穿它们，浸透她的全身，激得她一阵阵久长的寒颤，朝她尖声叫道：「我就是恐惧！」

她失声大叫。无人听见。她挣扎着到了门边。

紧邻的隔间里是她的女仆。起初她们对她莫名其妙的出逃大惑不解，其后又为她表现出的反常躁怒忧惧不已；此时一见到她，两人都急忙抢上前来。

===Original===
Salt Lake was the first possible asylum. There, weeks later, Leilah arose from one of those attacks, which, for lack of a better term, has been called brain fever.

Like fire, fever may consume, it does not necessarily obliterate. The past remained. But in that lassitude which fever leaves, Leilah was able to consider it with a wearied certainty that no immediate effort could be required of her then.

“Forget,” some considerate and subliminal self admonished. “Forget.”

Even in sleep she could not always do that. But though she could not forget the past, she could, she believed, barricade herself against it. The idea was suggested by the local sheet in which she found an item about neighbourly Nevada. The item hung a hammock for her thoughts, rested her mentally, unrolled a carpet for the returning steps of health.
===Chinese===
盐湖城是第一个可以栖身的避难所。数周之后，蕾拉在那里从一场发作中缓了过来——这类病症，因没有更贴切的名称，一向被唤作脑热病。

热病如火，可以蚀骨销形，却未必抹除一切。往昔仍在。但在热病留下的那种疲惫怠惰里，蕾拉得以怀着一种倦怠的笃定去审视它：此刻还不必向她索取什么立时的努力。

「忘掉吧，」某个体贴入微的、潜意识里的自我如此劝诫，「忘掉吧。」

即便在睡梦中，她也未必总能做到。然而尽管忘不掉过去，她相信，她总可以筑垒自守，把它拒之门外。这个念头来自当地报纸，她在上面读到一则关于睦邻内华达的简讯。那则简讯替她的思绪张起一张吊床，让她的精神得以歇息，为健康渐归的脚步铺开一条地毯。

===Original===
Verplank, meanwhile, misdirected at Los Angeles, reached San Francisco. Learning there that a party of three women had, that morning, at the last moment, embarked on the Samoa packet; learning also that of these women the central figure projected, or seemed to project, Leilah’s silhouette, he wired for his yacht and sailed away in pursuit. But an accident supervening, the packet reached Samoa before him. When Verplank got there the boat was gone. Still in pursuit he started for the austral seas. There, the mistake discovered, hope for the time abandoned him and he landed in Melbourne, ignorant that the supremely surgical court of Nevada was amputating him from his wife.

In matters of this solemnity, the Nevada statutes require that one of the parties to the operation shall have resided for six months within the state. But at Carson, the capital, a town that has contrived to superpose the Puritan aspect of a New England village on the vices of a Malay port, in this city Leilah learned that statutes so severe were not enacted for such as she.

The information, tolerably consoling, was placed before her by a young Jew who, as she alighted from the train, divined her errand, addressed her with easy Western informality, put a card in her hand, offered his services, telling her as he did so that if she retained him he would have her free in no time, in three months, in less. It was a mere matter of money, he explained, and, what he did not explain, a mere matter of perjury as well, the perjury of local oafs ready to swear to whatever they were paid for, ready to testify for instance that they had known anybody for any required length of time. But the Jew in divining Leilah’s errand divined too her loyalty. In speaking of fees, he kept manoeuvres and methods to himself.

Leilah, repelled yet beguiled, succumbed. The Jew was retained and in a wretched inn her things were unpacked. At once a rain of days began, long, loveless days in which she tried to starve her thoughts into submission and bear the cross that had been brought.
===Chinese===
与此同时，在洛杉矶被引错了方向的弗普兰克到了旧金山。他在那里得知，当天早晨最后一刻，有三名女子登上了开往萨摩亚的邮船；又得知这几名女子中居中的那个身影，投出的——或似乎投出的——正是蕾拉的侧影。于是他电召自己的游艇，扬帆追赶。不料酒生意外，邮船先他抵达萨摩亚。等弗普兰克赶到，船已开走。他继续追赶，驶入南方海域。在那里，误会终被揭穿，希望一时间弃他而去，他在墨尔本登岸，殊不知内华达那座刀法至为精妙的法院，正把他从妻子身上截除下去。

在此等大事上，内华达的法律规定：手术双方之一须在本州居住满六个月。但在州府卡森城——这座硬是把新英格兰村庄的清教面貌叠加在马来港口的恶习之上的城市——蕾拉却得知，如此严苛的法令，并非为她这样的人生而设。

这个相当宽慰人心的消息，是由一个年轻的犹太人摆到她面前的。她一下火车，他便猜中了她的来意，以西部人随和的不拘礼数跟她搭话，把一张名片塞进她手里，主动揽下这桩生意，还说只要她聘请他，用不了多久——三个月，兴许还用不了——就能还她自由之身。那不过是钱的问题，他解释道；而他没有解释的是，那也不过是个伪证问题——本地蠢汉们的伪证，收了钱便什么誓都肯发，譬如随时可以作证说，他们认识某人已有任何所需的时日。不过那犹太人既猜中了蕾拉的来意，也猜中了她的操守。谈到酬金时，他对那些手腕门道只字不提。

蕾拉既嫌恶又受诱惑，终于让了步。犹太人受了聘，在一家破败的客栈里，她的行李被一一打开。随即，一场日子的大雨落了下来——漫长的、没有爱的日子，她在其中试图把自己的念头饿到驯服，好背起那副被送来的十字架。

===Original===
The effort was not very satisfactory. The reason why she should have a cross and why it should be borne had never even to her devout mind been adequately explained. Hitherto she had not required any explanation and not unnaturally perhaps since she had had no cross to bear. The dogma that she in common with the rest of humanity must suffer because of the natural propensities of beings that never were, she had accepted as only such dogmas can be accepted, on faith. But in the dismal solitudes of Carson, faith faded, the dogma seemed absurd.

Then suddenly that which in her ignorance she took to be chance, supplied a superior view. While waiting in a shop for a slovenly clerk to do up a package, she looked at a shelf on which were some books﻿—frayed, bedrabbled, secondhand. Among them was a treatise on metallurgy, another on horse-breeding, a string of paper covered novels and the Vidya.

The title, which conveyed nothing, for that reason attracted. At random she opened the book. A paragraph sprang at her:

> “From debility to strength, from strength to power, from power to glory, from glory to perfection, from plane to plane, in an evolution proceeding from the outward to the inward, from the material to the spiritual, from the spiritual to the divine, such is the destiny of the soul.”
===Chinese===
这番苦功收效平平。凭什么她该有一副十字架，凭什么这十字架该由她来背——即便对她那颗虔诚的心，这问题也从未得到过妥善的解释。此前她并不需要任何解释，这也许并不奇怪，因为她压根无架可背。那条教义说，她与全人类一样，必须因一些从来不曾存在过的生灵的天性而受苦；她对这教义的接受，只能是对这类教义唯一可能的接受方式——凭信仰接受。可是在卡森城凄凉的荒寂里，信仰褪了色，教义显得荒诞。

就在这时，那件她因无知而当作机缘的东西，送来了一种更高的眼界。在一家铺子里等一个邋遢店员打包的当口，她望向一个摆着几本书的架子——磨损的、拖脏变色的二手书。其中有一本论冶金的小册子，一本讲育马的书，一串纸面小说，还有 the Vidya（梵语，义为「明／智」）。

书名于她全无意义，正因如此才引人。她信手翻开。一段文字朝她扑面而来：

> 「从孱弱到力量，从力量到权能，从权能到荣耀，从荣耀到圆满，从一个层面到另一个层面，在一场由外而内、由物而灵、由灵而神的演进之中——这便是灵魂的命运。」

===Original===
Leilah turned a page. Another paragraph leaped out.

> “There is not an accident in our lives, not a sorrow, a misfortune, a catastrophe, a happiness that is not due to our own conduct in this existence or in a previous one. In accordance with the nature of our deeds there are thrown about us the tentacles of pain or the arms of joy.”

But the slovenly clerk was approaching. Leilah closed the book, asked the price, paid for it, paid for the other purchase and went back to the inn where during the rest of the day she read the drama of the soul, the story of its emanation from the ineffable, of its surrender to desire, of its fall into matter, of its birth and rebirth in the mansions of life which are death, of the persistence there of its illusory joys, of the recurrences of its unenlightening trials, until, at last, some memory returning of what it had been when it was other than what it had become, it learns at last to conquer desire and accomplish its own release.
===Chinese===
蕾拉翻过一页。又一段文字跃然而出。

> 「我们的生命里没有一次意外，没有一桩悲伤、不幸、灾祸或幸福，不是缘于我们今生或前世的作为。依照我们业行的性质，缠绕在我们周身的，或是痛苦的触须，或是欢愉的手臂。」

可那邋遢的店员走过来了。蕾拉合上书，问了价钱，付了书钱，付了另一样买物的钱，回到客栈。当天余下的时光里，她读起了那部灵魂的戏剧：它如何自不可言说者流溢而出，如何向欲望缴械，如何堕入物质，如何在那些名为生的死亡之宅里一世又一世地诞生，如何在其中固守它虚幻的欢愉，如何一轮又一轮地经历那些无从启明的试炼，直到最后，某种记忆归来——记起它未变成如今这般之前的本来面目——它才终于学会征服欲望，成就自身的解脱。

===Original===
The drama, however old, was new to Leilah, and at first not very clear. But beneath it was a chain of causality, the demonstration that this life is the sum of many others, the harvest after the sowing, and, joined to the demonstration were corollaries and deductions which showed that sorrow, when rightly viewed, is not a cross but a gift, a boon granted to the privileged.

It was a little before she mastered the idea. When she had, the novelty of it impressed. At the back of the Vidya was a list of cognate works. She wired to San Francisco for them. Shortly they came, and in their companionship the rain of long, loveless days fell by.

Ultimately she sat on a high chair. An oaf asked her questions. Others testified. On the morrow a paper was brought her. It had on it a large seal, the picture of a big building, words that were engrossed, others in script.

She was free.
===Chinese===
这出戏剧无论多么古老，对蕾拉却是新的，起初也不甚了了。但在它底下是一条因果之链，是一个证明：此生乃许多前生之总和，是播种之后的收成。与这证明相连的还有种种推论与演绎，表明悲伤若看得得当，并不是十字架，而是一件礼物，一件授予受眷顾者的恩惠。

她稍费了些时日才吃透这个观念。一旦吃透，其新奇便令她震动。the Vidya 书后附着一份同类著作的书单。她发电报到旧金山去购。不久书便寄到，在它们的陪伴下，那场漫长而没有爱的日子之雨，淅淅沥沥地落了过去。

最终她坐上一把高椅。一个蠢汉向她提问。另一些人出庭作证。次日，一份文书送到了她手里。上面盖着一枚大大的火印，印着一座大楼的图样，有些字是工整誊写的，有些是手写的。

她自由了。

===Original===
The knowledge brought no exultation. It was a hostage to joy, one of the many that she was to give.

Meanwhile she had written to Violet Silverstairs telling her that she had separated from Verplank, and asking might she join her. The answer, which was cabled, told her to come. That day she started.

The town house of the Earls of Silverstairs is in Belgrave Square. There are worse places. But to the American countess the discomforts of the residence were not to be endured. After one season she declined to put up with them. Pending an entire modernisation of the house, she and Silverstairs migrated to Paris, where they took an apartment, and a very charming one, in the Rue François Premier.

In this apartment Leilah was made to feel that she was with friends, one of whom, however, could not get over the fact that she could not get at the facts in the matter.
===Chinese===
这份认知并未带来丝毫欢欣。它是交给欢乐的人质，是她日后还要交出的许多人质之一。

在此期间她已写信给维奥莱特·西尔弗斯泰尔斯，说自己已与弗普兰克分居，问可否去投她。回电叫她去。当天她就动身了。

西尔弗斯泰尔斯历代伯爵的城里府邸在贝尔格雷夫广场。比那更差的地方有的是。可对这位美国伯爵夫人来说，宅邸里的种种不便是忍无可忍的。熬过一个社交季，她便拒绝再将就下去。趁全宅彻底翻修的当口，她与西尔弗斯泰尔斯迁往巴黎，在弗朗索瓦一世街租下一套公寓——一套相当迷人的公寓。

在这套公寓里，蕾拉感到自己置身朋友之间；只是其中一位却始终迈不过一道坎——她竟然探不到这件事的实情。

===Original===
“See here, Leilah,” Violet Silverstairs said aggrievedly, not once, but fifty times, “it is downright mean of you to keep me in the dark. What was it that he did? Tell me.”

The lady had known Verplank, as she had known Leilah, ever since she had known anybody. They had grown up together. Though not related by blood, they were by choice, which is sometimes thicker. In the circumstances it was perhaps but natural that she should call it mean, perhaps but human that she should be aggrieved.

The puzzle of the situation she put before her husband.

“What do you suppose it can be?” she asked.

But Silverstairs had no surmises to hazard.
===Chinese===
「你听着，蕾拉，」维奥莱特·西尔弗斯泰尔斯委屈地说，说了不止一遍，而是五十遍，「你把我蒙在鼓里，实在太刻薄了。他到底干了什么？告诉我。」

这位女士认识弗普兰克，正如她认识蕾拉，从她认识任何人起就认识了。他们是一起长大的。虽无血缘之亲，却有金兰之谊，而后者有时更浓于血。处此情形，她称之为刻薄也许不过出于天性，她委屈生气也许不过出于人情。

她把这一团疑云摆到了丈夫面前。

「你说能是什么呢？」她问。

可西尔弗斯泰尔斯无意妄加揣测。

===Original===
“It must be something quite too dreadful,” Violet continued. “One of those things, don’t you know, that are said to change your whole life. She just sits about and reads queer books.”

“Queer books!” Silverstairs surprisedly repeated.

“Yes, books that tell of planes and rounds and cycles and chains of lives and rebirths and redeaths. She believes in them, too. She told me so.”

Silverstairs tugged at his moustache. “She might as well believe in the music of the spheres.”

Violet looked at her lord. She loved him as certain delicately organized women do love men who are merely robust. But her affection did not warp her judgment. She knew that within his splendid physique was a spirit, valiant perhaps, but obtuse.
===Chinese===
「那准是件可怕透顶的事，」维奥莱特接着说，「就是那种，你懂的，据说会改变你一生的事情。她就整天闲坐着，读些古怪的书。」

「古怪的书！」西尔弗斯泰尔斯诧异地重复道。

「是啊，讲什么层面、轮次、周期，讲生命之链、转生与再死的书。她还信那些。她亲口跟我说的。」

西尔弗斯泰尔斯捻着髭须。「那她还不如去信天体音乐。」

维奥莱特望着她的夫君。她爱他，正如某些天性纤巧的女人爱那些纯粹体魄雄健的男人那样爱着他。但她的深情并不歪曲她的判断。她知道，在那副堂堂仪表之内住着一个灵魂，或许勇毅，却迟钝得很。

===Original===
“Well, why not?” she retorted. “Let a microphone receive from a steel plate the reflection of a star, and sounds are emitted, tones peculiar to the star itself. Those of the sun are blatant. Those of Arcturus are like little bells. Those of Sirius are as sobs from a zither. Everybody knows that. Why shouldn’t she believe in the music of the spheres?”

“Gammon!” cried this man who at Eton and Christ Church had abundantly acquired everything which is most useless. “I never heard such rot.”

“I daresay, but that is not the point. The point is that it is no joke to her.”

Nor was it. Leilah at first refused to go anywhere, to see anyone, to be present when there were guests. But Violet, declaring that she would have no moping in her diggings, forced her. It was very reluctantly that Leilah acceded. After a while she did so as a matter of course. Finally, as was inevitable, she accepted invitations elsewhere.

It was what Violet had aimed at, though not at all at the result. Yet that, Leilah, who had come to believe in karma, afterward regarded as fate.
===Chinese===
「哦，为什么不能信？」她反唇相讥。「让一支麦克风从一块钢板上接收一颗星辰的回响，便会有声音发出，那是星辰独有的音调。太阳的声音喧嚣震耳。大角星的声音像一串小铃。天狼星的声音像齐特琴上的一声呜咽。这是人人都知道的事。凭什么她就不能信天体音乐？」

「胡说八道！」这位在伊顿和基督堂学院把天下最无用的学问学了个十足的人物嚷道。「我从没听过这种浑话。」

「也许吧，可那不是重点。重点是，这对她来说不是玩笑。」

确实不是。起初蕾拉哪里都不肯去，谁都不肯见，有客在座也不肯露面。但维奥莱特声称她的窝里容不得闷闷不乐，硬逼她去。蕾拉勉为其难地依了。过了一阵，出去便成了自然而然的事。到后来，不出所料，别处的邀约她也接受了。

这正是维奥莱特所谋求的，尽管谋求的全然不是这样的结果。而这结果，后来被已然信了业的蕾拉看作命运。

===Original===
Presently, it so fell about that at one dinner she had at her left a man whom she did not know, whose name she had not caught and with whom, during the preliminary courses, she had not exchanged a word. As the dinner progressed, cigarettes were served. Twice she refused them. The second time, as she turned again to the man at her right, she heard a cry, across the table she saw a face, the eyes staring, the features elongated. At once there was an uproar, behind her there was a crash, she was torn bodily from her chair, a piece of tapestry had been thrown about her and in it she was rolled on the floor by the man whom she did not know.

Probably, at no dinner, anywhere, had a woman suffered such indignities. She was so telling herself when she realised, as she immediately did realise, that the man and others who had joined him, were but occupied in saving her life. Her dress had caught fire and it was in this flaming fashion, hurled on the floor by a stranger and there brutalised by him, that she made the acquaintance of Count Kasimiérz Barouffski.

The sack of her costume forced her to return to the Rue François Premier, where at five o’clock the next day, Barouffski appeared. He appeared the day following, the day after, the day after that.

These attentions Violet Silverstairs viewed with suspicion.
===Chinese===
不久，事情就这样巧了：某次晚宴上，她左手边坐着一位她不认识的男子，名字没听清，开场几道菜的时间里也未曾交谈一句。宴至中途，香烟端了上来。她两次谢绝。第二次回过头去再看右手边那位男士时，她听见一声惊叫，隔着桌子看见一张脸，双目圆睁，五官拉长。霎时间一片大乱，她身后哗啦一声巨响，她整个人被从椅子上拽了下来，一块花毯兜头罩住，裹在毯子里的她被那个她不认识的男子按在地板上翻滚。

大约天底下没有哪一顿晚宴上，有女人受过这样的屈辱。她正这样想着，随即便意识到——她立刻就意识到了——那男子和加入他的其他人，都只是在忙着救她的命。她的裙子着了火。就是这样，裹在一团火焰里，被一个陌生人掼倒在地板上、被他粗手粗脚地摆布着，她结识了卡西米耶日·巴罗夫斯基（Kasimiérz Barouffski）伯爵。

那身裙袍毁得精光，逼得她回到弗朗索瓦一世街。第二天下午五点，巴罗夫斯基登门造访。次日他又来了，再次日又来，日复一日。

这些殷勤，维奥莱特·西尔弗斯泰尔斯看在眼里，满腹狐疑。

===Original===
“I verily believe,” she said to Leilah, “that it was that polecat who set you on fire, and if he did no one can convince me that he did not do it on purpose.”

“Violet!”

“That’s right, fly at me. I thought you would. Are you going to take him?”

In an elaborate drawing room in the Rue François Premier the two women were having tea. Leilah, without replying, raised her cup.

Violet cocked an eye at her. “One would have thought that you had had enough of matrimony. But perhaps your intentions are not honourable.”
===Chinese===
「我千真万确地相信，」她对蕾拉说，「放火烧你的就是那只臭鼬，他要是放的火，谁也别想让我相信他不是故意的。」

「维奥莱特！」

「这就对了，冲我来了。我就知道你会。你打算要他吗？」

在弗朗索瓦一世街一间陈设考究的客厅里，两位女子正在用茶。蕾拉不答，举起了茶杯。

维奥莱特斜睨她一眼。「人家还以为你把婚姻受够了哩。不过嘛，也许你的用意并不体面。」

===Original===
Leilah reddened. “Violet!” she again exclaimed.

“My dear,” Lady Silverstairs resumed, “remember that you are no longer in the States. England is the most hypocritical country in Europe. America is the most hypocritical country in the world. That is what we call progress. But France being old-fashioned and behind the times is not censorious. I admit, I used to be. But I am not censorious any longer. I am not because any such *état d’âme* while advanced is not becoming.

“I am an old married woman,” added the lady who was not twenty-two. “But if I were not, if for instance I were like you, free, independent and not a fright, and I had to choose between love and matrimony, it would not take me a moment to decide. Not one.”

Leilah put down her cup. “Of course it would not. If you had it to do again you would marry Silverstairs and you would marry for love. That is over for me, over forever.”
===Chinese===
蕾拉红了脸。「维奥莱特！」她又喊了一声。
